DOES THE WELL-BEING OF A 'MINN

INCREASE WITH ITS SIZE?

Sir,—Mr. C. E. Parker is evidently a firm believer in the borough's motto, "Deus dat Incrementum"—"God giveth the increase." But while he may pin his faith to this maxim, I would venture to reminclaiim that there is an older, and probably truer, saying that "God helps those who help themselves." What ails the proposal that Warrington should help itself by endeavouring to make the best of those natural industrial advantages it possesses, and which an enterprising Clrporation has devel- oped to such an extent ~.kat they need only to be known for others ta 13e induced to come in and share in the beneL.s with those who are at , the present time reaping them? - Mr. Parker discusses at great length his self- put question—" Does the well-being of a town increase with its size?"—and he arrives at the conclusion that it is an illusory: theory to sup- pose that it does. How is it, I would ask, that most towns are so anxious to extend their boun- daries as opportunity offers? Mr. Parker need not go out of Warrington to find a concrete case where expansion has had financial advantages. The last extension of the borough ultimately resulted in such an increase in the rateable value as to enable the Corporation to make a substantial reduction in the rates or in enabling the Corporation to keep the rates lower than they would otherwise have been. The introduc- tion of new f•orks within the borough boundary must necessarily have a similar effect, to say nothing of the direct benefits which would fol- low in their train to shopkeepers, property owners, and, to please Mr. Parker, landowners. Mr. Parker, we know, is a Progressive, but on this question of town expansion he shows himself to be astonishingly conservative. No one, probably, knoit,s.hetter than he that War- rington occupies- a somewhat unique position from an industrial point of view. Why should he begrudge others being made acquainted with the fact? ADVERTISE.

GREENGROCERS AND ORGANISATION.

We have received a further letter from Mr. Walter Byfield, greengrocer, 155, Marsh House- lane, regretting that his former letter upon the need for organisation in the greengrocers' trade has not called forth an adequate response. There are, he says, a few exceptions to the indiffer- ence generally shown in which tradeerac:t are intent upon raising the tone of the trade. He suggests the formation of an association, and says that as soon as sufficient names have been sent in a meeting will be called through "The Examiner." The evils he has described, he says, can be remedied. Therefore, he concludes, let us set to work at once and do it, and not sit like the old woman upon the doorstep wringing her hands, saying, "It can't be done; it can't be done." We had better get inside. If any care still to sit upon the doorstep the result will be they will just simply be trampled upon, because we have become convinced that it'earr be done,: and that everyone concerned will be better for the doing of it. Those who wish to become members may intimate their desire to do so by sending in their names to any of the addresses below : WALTER BYFIELD, 155, Marsh House-lane, Warrington. JOHN LOCKETT,

Winwick-road. JOHN BELL, Willis-street. A. SAMPSON, Manchester-road.

NOBLE IMPERIALISM.

Sir,—ln the "War or Peace?" addresses deliv- ered recently in Warrington by Mr. H. E. Brown, great care was taken to point out that the real struggle in life, if rightly understood, was not between man and man, but between man and Nature. The historic phrase, "The law of the survival of the fittest," had been grossly misunderstood, and, in consequence, mischievously applied to human affairs. It was more truthful to say that the law of life is co- operation, and not struggle. To-day I have been reading of the splendid example of co-operation which is worth record- ing. It deals with the work of irrigation in our Indian Empire. The article is headed "I The Conquest of Nature." It says " There is no more satisfactory part of our rule in Egypt and India than the work that has been done by the Government Engineer. The great Dam of Assouan has often been described, and it is difficult to exaggerate the benefits that it has conferred upon Egypt, but the irrigation works of India dwarfs into insignificance what has been accomplished in Egypt. Not only has a vast area of land been reclaimed, but the scourge of famine has been mitigated to a large extent, and millions of lives saved. The fain- : ines experienced in our great Eastern possessions are so terrible in their effects that whole popula- tions have been depopulated. Even so late as ; 1900 the death-roll in that great famine reached over 3,000,000, and 65,000,000 people (almost twice the population of Great Britain) were brought to the verge of starvation. The Government of India have spent X 3.9,000,000 in irrigation works and reclaimed 22,500,000 acres. The money spent, apart from the preservation of life, yields an . interest of 7 per cent. About 53,000,000 acres of land are now irrigated in India, and much of this land was virtual desert before the irrigation works were completed. At the present moment in the Punjaub there are 1,326 miles of main t, State canals in course of construction, and 4,936 miles of distributing canals. These figures, even as they stand, are sufficiently impressive, but translated into terms of poverty relieved and wealth produced, they would lead us to heap honours upon the engineers who devised ' and carried out schemes so productive of good I I for millions of natives in our Indian Empire." I will leave this vivid picture to speak for itself. Whatever defects may characterise British rule in India, this combined work of the engineer and capitalist provides a magnificent example of a just and noble ILYROLD GRAY.

THE CATHOLIC LECTURES.

Sir,—! cannot let Mr. Ogden's last letter pass without some comment. It is a direct insult, not Jnly to myself, but to those interested readers who have followed the controversy, naturally, with the intention of coming to some settled z!onclnsion regarding the subject which was under discussion. He has treated my last letter with. undisguised contempt. He charges me, sir, with being Very personal" I defy Mr. Ogden to show me one single passage in my published correspondence (which is, in his estimation, personal. His last letter calls for iomething more, than personalities, but I will play the man, and restrain myself, fully con- vinced that I was justified in taking up the con- troversy with such a weak exponent of the Bap- t;st"faith. If I have really affeeted his feelings I am sorry; but, sir, I certainly think that his Jew of abusive language has been far stronger than my ,own. I have never used in,my correspondence such words as "ignorant," " impudent," "coward." Where ignorance is bliss it is folly to be wise. Mr. Ogden thinks it wiser to conclude the con- troversy, not with a last appeal to his theologi- cal study, but with a scathing and uncalled-for attack upon myself. He speaks, sir—referring to myself—of "a coward's pluck," who hides behind the fence. The heat judges of who has displayed the most pluck are those readers who have followed the controversy. I leave the ver- dict to them. I fight my own battle; I do not ask for any relief; I do not, nor will I, recognise my weakness, as Mr. Ogden does. He recognises his own weakness by appealing to someone more intellectual than himself to take his place in the controversy. His reply to my last letter is an attempt to thrust upon the public the old bubbles, " Papacy " and "Priestcraft," bubbles which ' have burst long ago, and are only revived by Free Church ministers, as a last resource to retain the respect and reverence of their parish- ioners. One thing.,l do recognise. and that is, Free Churchmen will not listen to reason, nor will they- admit defeat.. Theirs, is a mythical Church, the foundation of which is a composi- tion of sand and dust. Nobody knows what they do believe; they believe there was a Saviour, a Redeemer, but His mysterious teach- ing they will not accept. Hence their hostile attitude towards the Bride of Christ, the Catho- lic Church.

Everywhere Catholics are beset with hostility or ridicule, founded on ignorance. If Catholics were able to reply to the taunts and jeers to which they were subjected by showing that their critics were labouring under a misconception as to Catholic teaching and practice, and if they could show the immense spiritual advantages derivable from a proper knowledge and appre- ciation of Catholic truths, they would break down the ramparts of ignorance and turn those outside the Church from hostile critics into friendly inquirers. The Catholic Church has nothing to fear and everything to gain frem an unbiassed inquiry into the doctrines of her Faith. Those inquirers who throw aside their prejudices would find in Catholicity the greatest 1 civilising and refining influence which the world I had ever known—a power which, by showing mankind the higher purposes of human exist- ence, established ideals as far above the sordid teachings of materialistic philosophy as intel- lectual man was above the brute creation by which he was surrounded. One of the most difficult things in the world is to convince a Free Churchman of the inviolable sanctity of objective truth. He thinks that it does not matter what you believe; and if the Protestant mind were not so woefully illogical, he would conclude from this premise which he has laid down for his guidance that God never made any revelation at all. Indeed, towards this lamentable conclusion Protestantism is slowly but surely moving. Men refused to listen to the Catholic Church because notwithstanding the spread of educa- tion they were still ignorant of the greatest truth of history—the fact of a Church which, although established two thousand years ago, was still animated by the same undying vitality and doctrines which were eternal because they were true. In conclusion, sir, I would like Mr. Ogden to bear in mind that whenever he attempts to ridicule Catholic practices, he will always find the mysterious "J.D." ready, aye, only too willing, to cross swords with him again. J. D. Warrington, .May sth, 1914.

Sir,—Romanists seem to think themselves so wise, and their Church so infallible, that they must always be right and everyone else wrong; it never occurs to them that it is possible for any other system to be right but theirs. Mr. Richardson seems to be no exception to this rule; and 'appears to be obsessed by the idea that the greatest personality in earth or heaven is the Pope of Rome. He writes of teaching, claiming for his Church the sole right to do so, and expecting our submissive acceptance of all he puts forward. Teachers always have text- books, but our friend does not disclose his or say whether he has got one. He is very wise about the true Church being the voice of God, but I think he fails to prove the Roman Catho- lic Church to be the true Church. We claim for Protestant Christians the right of a teaching Church, whose text-book and guide is the Bible, the pure Word of God. We sit at the feet of Jesus, and learn of Him, and by this test we are quite willing to be judged, and there is no better means of proving which is right than by a comparison of the teaching of the two Churches. •

T the Take the Churches. scheme nßtehdee mßpotmi oann s taught forgivenessin is obtained by penance, by money payment or physical endurance. The Protestant Church teaches the free salvation of the Cross, faith in which builds up the Christian's hope and illu- minates his darkest hours, and when the last ' stage is reached he launches out in full hope of the Resurrection and the Life. Faith to the Protestant Christian is a root of hope, which sinks deep into good ground, bringing forth a goodly tree, bearing the precious fruit of good deeds and actions. The true Christian's hope is not in his, own merits, but in the love of God and the redemption of His Son. What hope does the Roman Church hold out to its mem- bers? The dreary toil of working out a dreary role of penance to meet with an undefined period in purgatory at its close. lam sorry-that I cannot please Mr. Richard- son by seeing as he seen, but I cannot let either the priest or the Roman Church stand between me and my Creator. Certainly I have given sufficient grounds for denying the Roman Church the right to be the true Church. The issue is plain. Which is the greatest? The

PHIL SQUOD.

Sir,—Replying to my letter wherein I sug- gested as to what should be the nature of the )roposed Protestant Evidence Lectures, "Phil 3quod" makes as good a show as the weakness Jf his case will permit. He states fear of not ;iving me satisfaction. I wonder if he is sat- Lsfied? I am. He has failed. The considera- tion of one point from his reply to my second luestion will be sufficient.

He says: "The Bible- is the only infallible iuthority in the world." Certainly the Bible s God's Word, but as the written word may present different meanings to different men, and Is God could only have intended His meaning co be delivered to and accepted by men, it was therefore necessary, in order to expound that written word, for there to be an authorisel 'iving voice established, and endowed with the nature of unerring certitude. This nature of ;nfallible teaching could only have been given to men by God, and Christ gave this power to :he Church when He said to the Apostles: "He

that heareth you, heareth Me"; and "Go and teach all nations=Behold, I am with you all days, even to the consummation of, the world." If Christ had willed His message to be deliv- ered to the world by means of the Bible, surely He would have written or have commanded His Apostles to have written it. What evidence is there of such? The command He did give to His Apostles was "Go and teach." , The true Church, then, must teach, and this "Phil Squod" admits by his evasive answer. A com- parison between question and answer shows this. _ .

I asked, "Can Protestantism give proofs of haying Divine authority to teach, and show that it . fulfils Christ's command to 'Teach all nations,' if it could .be shown that it does teach?" and our friend replies These ques- tions may be answered together, and I may say that in addition to the Scriptural command is the splendid success of the Christian doctrine proclaimed from the" Protestant point of view." He then tries to shift the ground of argument by asking: "Why are Romanist countries so backward compared with England?" • • I must remind him that the country which rivals or outstrips England in material prosperity is a Catholic one, namely, Belgium. However, the reason why England and Belgium are so pros- Perous materially, compared with such as Sweden (Protestant) and 'Spain, (Catholic) is because of the great mineral wealth of the first- named countries: But material wealth is no test of spiritual health. of being in possession of the truth. A more appropriate question for our friend would have been: "If the Catholic Church is the true one, considering we are so highly edu- cated and enlightened, why do not Englishmen join that Church?" Ignoring the fact that a great many are coining into the Church, the reasons are: (I) Because of the appalling indif- ference and spiritual blindness caused by mate- rial prosperity, or by indulgence in pleasure; (2) or because they, are misled and blinded by calumnious literature, or by non-Catholic lee- tut•ers, unable and .unwilling to state the Catbo. lie case. _ _

On the other hand it is to be feared that there are a great many who believe the Catholic Church to be the true one, but will not follow the light of conscience, or there are others who suspect that such is the case, but cease their investigations, which, if pursued, they believe would compel them to become Catholics if they were true to their consciences.

_ If "Phil Squod" then would prove flip truth of Protestantism, he must give far bet-mr evi: dence than he has yet put forward. He must prove that it teaches "I say." "M.A. (Cantab.)" has shirked the teaching question altogether so far. His last letter proves noth- ing. The anti-Popery argument is of no value as proving the truth of Protestantism. 1. RICHARDSON. Buttermarket-street.

This correspondence is now closed.—Ths EDITOR.

SUNDAY CLOSING.

Sir,--The Bill now before Parliament, which deals with Sunday closing, is a very moderate measure. It proposes to reduce the hours of sale of intoxicating liquor on Sunday from two to one hour at midday, and from four to two hours in the evening in the provinces, and from five to three in London. It will increase the distance for travellers from three to six miles, and will give power to licensing justices to close all public-houses in their district. The National 'trade Defence League is, of course (as a league composed of the ,alcoholic trade) protesting against this Bill, and at a meeting held in Manchester on May Ist, Mr. Harold Smith was the speaker. He is reported in "The Examiner" of May 2 as having said: "The Bill was unnecessary, undemocratic, and made no attempt to solve the problem of drunkenness." Well, sir, I do not agree with him on the last point, because drunkenness is caused by the taking of alcoholic drink, and if we as a nation can restrict the hours of sale even by three per week, we are sure to deal in that way with drunkenness. On Sunday last, at 10.10 to 10.15 p.m., in Horsemarket-street, there was a drunken brawl, fighting &c., be- tween soldiers and civilians, which I venture to submit would not have taken place if public- .uses had only been open two hours in the avening instead of four. I would like this moderate measure placed upon our Statute Book at once, because the larger Government Temperance Bill, to which we are looking for- ward, will probably have to rely upon the Par- liament Act, which, of course, takes time. 8, Willis-street.

lllouses

BENT: T. PARKES.

RUNCORN DISTRICT COUNCIL

MEETING.

Provision Agtinst Fire.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tues- day, Mr. C. E. Linaker presiding. Mr. Arthur IL Leece, assistant surveyor to the 'Council, wrote applying for an increase of salary. The matter was referred to the Highway Executive Committee.

The Weston Parochial Committee reported an- pointing Mr. J. B. Chadwick chairman for the year, and Mr. .T. Tudor vice,chairman. Messrs. Whitby, Ellison and Moore, for Weaver Ward, and Messrs. Aspey, Hazlehurst and Earle, for Weston Ward, were appointed reference sub- committees to deal with matters arising in con- nection with conversions and sanitary improve- ments in the parish. A suggestion from the Surveyor of the Runcorn Urban District Coun- cil that fire hydrants should be placed on the new 6-inch water main now being laid by the Urban Council through Weston, was considered, and it was resolved that five hydrants be laid down at a cost of £3 per hydrant. It was re- solved that the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company be asked if they would grant to the District Council the use of their fire brigade whenever fires may occur in the parish of Weston, and if so, upon iihat terms.—The Public Health Sur- veyor reported.tha,t a shed had been erected at Mrs. Campbell's, Weaver Farm, without the submission of a plan and contrary to the by- law-s. It was resolved that it be a recommenda- tion to the District Council that Mrs. Campbell be required to show cause why the building should not be pulled down. The report was confirmed.

THE MAN WHO WINS.

Some men seem to have all the luck. If there are .any good things going these men get them. They can make other people do their will. They are leaders. If they are workmen, they get a foreman's job. If they are bachelors, they win the 'prettiest girl. They have the power of in- fluencing people. The same thing is true of women. Some women have the charm which makes all the men seek them out and try to win them; others are always neglected. .

- This is not luck. It is due to a personal gift— vitality. Men and women of this sort are never weak, puny invalids. They may not be big, but they are full of life and energy. The whole thing is a matter of good nerves and full-blooded manliness.

Everyone would wish to be' like this, and the qualities which make for vitality are purely a matter of health. By building up the blood, nerves and spine; sleeplessness, want of energy, bashfulness, weakness of the back, stooping shoulders, indigestion, headache, and the low- spirited, ineffectual sort of presenqe which really comes from weakness, can all be got rid of. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have Made many weak- kneed tired men vigorous' and healthy, and many pale, flat-chested women plump, rosy and improving their blood,and tojting up their nerves. Your dealer is almost • sure to have Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People on sale; if not, Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., 46, Holborn- viaduct, London, will send a. box post free for 2s. 9d., or six for 13s. 9d. If you would like to read more before trying them, write a post-card to-day to the Post Dept., 46, Holborn-viaduct, London, for an instructive book on The Nerves and their Needs," sent free.

LABOUR'S MAY DAY.

Demoistration by the Workers of

the Borough. SPEAKERS & TORY METHODS.

Warrington's Labour forces for the first time celebrated May Day by a demonstration on Saturday, when several well-known speakers stated their propaganda from two lorries near the Old Mill at Bridge Foot.

The afternoon's proceedings were Prefaced by a procession, in which numerous banners were unfurled, and when the processionists marched to the music of two bands. A striking feature was the appearance of a "knight in shining armour," who, mounted upon a fine steed, re- presented the Sheet Metal Workers' Union. , Mr: Charles Dukes, secretary of the Trades and Labour Council, presided at one meeting, and was supported by Messrs. Dan. Irving and R. Wellhead, whilst Mr. Eph. Randles, presi- dent of the Trades and Labour Council, the chairman at the. other meeting, was 'supported by Messrs. Albert Lees, Gavan Duffy, and T. IL. Dunford. . .

TRIBUTE TO THE MAYOR. Mr. CHARLES Dmas said they had just suc- cessfully terminated a nine, weeks' struggle with Messrs. Fletcher, Russell's, for the elementary right to organise, Personally he did not blame the employers; he blamed the workers that at that late hour of the day it should be necessary to work for it. (year, hear.) My message to you men, declared Mr. Dukes, is that the same united front you have shown in the industrial field should be shown in ()tiler directions. It is, just as necessary that you should demonstrate . like solidarity when you meet the political expression -of those with whom you have been fighting. (Hear, hear.) We fought for nine weeks, and, with the exception of your Mayor, no man of in- fluence offered any Assistance until the tight was won.

• Mr. DAx IRVING, of the 8.5. P., said they were told that Socialism meant the tearing up of the churches, the abolition of• decdnt regula- tions in society as between men and women, and 'the breaking up of home life, but that was a false idea.

of the proprietors of "Punch."

the way to sending a representative to Imperial Parliament.

Mr. ALBERT LEES, of Manchester, said as they 'came out into the May sunshine so must they Come out into the social sunshine. And at this demonstration they were making a beginning. Things were improving, for they could see that the workers were at last looking to their own well-being. The spirit of these battles was an inherent characteristic of the workers under their present state. The representatives of law and order showed that their sympathies were with the employers of labour. Many of the trade union members had been imprisoned dur- ing the industrial conflicts for negligible offences. They were beaten because they had not the spirit to defend themselves when the opportunity came.

The Town Council.

He asked them to look at the constitution of the Warrington Town Council, and see if it represented the workers., The Council did not help the trade unionists. He did not say that If they had representatives of their own they would bring heaven or earth to them, but they would at least help towards it. Mr, T. H. Dingman, of the Dockers' Union, said that the immediate work of the trade hnionists should be the restoration of efficient organisation in their ranks. Everything had become distorted, and the workers were not. banded together. Mr. GAVON Duerr, in the course of a humorous speech, said that the Ulster problem had noth- ing to do with politics. However, he was not sorry that Sir Edward Carson had gone mad. As for the workers of Warrington being repre- sented in Parliament by a lawyer, a tripe dresser would be of more use to the workers. Briefly, says Hallam, "they suffered a system of oppres- sion under, a series of laws which were fitted for oppression, impoverishment and degradation of a people and a debasement in them of human nature itself, as ever proceeded from the per- verted ingenuity of man."

THE TEST OF GOOD GOVERNMENT. Interrupting this review for a moment, it may be asked: "What are the tests of good government?

"That it should give security to the gov- erned?"—The government of Ireland by Eng- land began by refusing to recognise in the 'native ;Irish any rights at all before the law. " That it should hold the balance evenly be- tween the various types of conscientious belief?" —The Government of England attempted to enforce the religion of a small alien minority by every conceivable penalty; it used religion as a cloak for destruction; it deliberately maintained religion as a symbol of racial division. "That it should temper justice with mercy?" —The English people may be said to have aimed at the complete extirpation of the native race, for, writes Sir Charles Bruce : • " The history of the English in Ireland for 700 years has confirmed the truth of the pro- position that in the treatment of the native population in conquered territories there are only two possible policies—extermination or amalgamation The policy of ex- termination having failed, it falls to consider. what has been done and what remains to be done to substitute a policy of amalgamation." The first fact, then, about England's proved fitness Or unfitness to govern Ireland is that England attempted to exterminate the native Irish and failed. It remains to be seen how, after the failure, she dealt with Ireland when that country was completely subject to. British rule.

A STUDY IN DIMENSIONS-THE SIZE OF

CANADA.

The Right Hon. R. L. Borden, dealing with the dimensions of the Dominion of Canada, re- cently asked his audience: " Do you realise how great a country Canada is If you _could pivot Canada upon US eastern seaboard, it would cdver the northern part of the Atlantic Ocean, the British Isles, Norway, Sweden, Denmark, Hol- land, Belgium, the northern part of France, the entire German Empire, and a considerable part of European Russia. A man who lives in Hali- fax, Nova Scotia, is a thousand miles farther away from Victoria, British Columbia, than he is from London."

CROWN ANEMONE.

Everybody who has any acquaintance with garden flowers knows well the value of the poppy or crown anemone, its easy culture, and the variety of colour which a mixed collection affords. By sowing in June and planting out the resulting seedlings in the autumn, flowers in Plenty may be had the following spring.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SOFT FEEDS.

1 Soft mash feeds giving best results are biscuit meal, bran (either dry or scalded). Sussex ground oats (especially for quick fattening), oatmeal, with the inilling by-products called pollards, thirds, sharps, &c., used for drying off purposes, and to these must be added during the autumn and winter months meat meal, bone meal, containing all the marrOw or gluten, or fresh cut green bone, but this must be given as fresh ,as possible, with the addition of the now popular dried clover and alfalfa scalded and mixed with the biscuit meals. And a cab- bage hung up in the scratching shed should effect a very great difference in the egg output. without resorting to condiments.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
A PRETTY ANNUAL.

Among the showiest of our hardy annuals are the Larkspurs, which will keep up a succession of bloom for many weeks in the summer. We have now a great variety of colour forms, and their culture is not at all difficult. Seeds may be sown now very thinly in warm, sheltered borders, in which the soil is fairly light. In the case of the taller forms it is well to support them lightly; otherwise rough weather may break off the flowering spikes.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THINNING SEEDLINGS.

31any cultivators have a weakness for failing to sufficiently thin their seedlings. If they think that by leaving a lot of plants jumbled together they are going to get more flowers,3,it is a mis- taken idea. Some annuals require more space to grow, on-account of their branching habit. It is a tedious. work thinning out plants, but if good-sized flowers are expected, thinning must be done when the seedlings are quite small, and another one later on when a better idea of the habit of the plant is seen.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE IVY.

Although Ivy is somewhat easy to grow it is slower growing than most climbers. Old walls and house walls that are decaying are most suitable for growing Ivy, because the:weather- beaten bricks enable the Ivy growths to cling better than on a smooth surface. In planting Ivy put small plants close to the wall pressing the stems close to the wall, and fix to the wall with a staple. When growth begins strip the leading shoot, which will induce lateral growths to spread out, and cling to the wall. Then in turn stop laterals, which will make a spreading close-clinging plant. The following year a con- siderable wall space will be covered. With established plants any cutting required mny be done now.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 9, 1914.

FARM AND GARDEN_

ivIAY PROSPECTS.

The farmer can take a more hopeful view of the outlook at the beginning of May than 'he did a month ago, says "The Times." The bright, Warm weather, of April served his pur- pose well, even although it would have been improved by occasional showers of -the type characteristic of the month. ,The sunshine and high temperatures atoned in substantial measure for' the absent rains, and acted as cor- rectives of the intermittent easterly winds and slight night frosts. The appearance of the landscape shows that vegetation suffered no harm, although growth was restricted from the lack of surface moisture, and it is only when one inquires into the workini of the heavy soils that the departure from the traditional alternation of sunshine and shower is found to have operated to the disadvantage of the hui- bandman. .At the beginning of last week the country presented an inspiring appearance, as full of promise as it was rich in colour. But May is a critical month in 'agrarian interests, not only because of its extreme meteorological vagaries, but also because in field and in garden vegetation is at a delicate stage of development that lays it open to weather influences for good or the reverse.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
A BULL FIGHT.

Two ferocious bulls were in pasture at Coven- ham Manor when one attacked the other. The bulls continuously thrust their horns into each other until one fell dead, pierced through the heart. The other bull, which was much in- jured, calmed down after its victory, but no one dared apprcach the bulls when they were fight- ing.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
LIME AND CHEESE-MAKING.

Lecturing at Bristol University on the Chem- istry of Cheese, Dr. Nierenstein said the pres- ence of lime in milk was very important for the manufacture of cheese. The countries and counties where cows grazed over limestone ground produced milk which yielded a good cheese. In America they actually added lime- stone to the soils in order to obtain good milk. When first he took up this question he asked: Why not add calcium to the cows' food? But that would not. work. The cow had to take up the calcium through the plant which it ate.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUL IN THE FOOT.

This is a term, says "Farm and Home," very loosely employed to cover any disease of the foot in which there is inflammation and subse- quent disintegration of structure. " Stinking" foul expresses a,condition in which there is a foul-smelling discharge associated with suppura- tion of the interdigital substance. In some cases the majority of the cows in a herd become affected one after another, and the disease is thought to be infectious, In a sense it is, since there is wound infection, but the original cause is due to the exposure of all to the same un- favourable conditions, which include the irrita- tion of wet and dirt. .In such a ease dryness and cleanliness are of the utmost importance, and instead of letting cows wade through a dirty yard to obtain 'water, it would be better to take the water to them. The first step in active treatment is to poultice the foot to clean and soften it, and then to remove all over-grown horn, or horn that is decayed, detached, or under which pus has burrowed, taking care to conserve anything that is sound. Afterwards, the wound should be treated with antiseptic and astringent agents, and the powerful caustics so often resorted to avoided. Each case should be treated on its merits, and in accordance with the indications of the moment, but a useful all- round dressing is a mixture of equal parts of butyr of antimony and tincture of myrrh. Fre- quent washing out by irrigation and syringing with a weak solution of carbolic acid are use- ful. •

• VALUE IN MILK RECORDS.

Breeders' of cattle who keep records of the , milk yielded by their Coivs' are being provided with increasing proof of the wisdom of the practice. The valni of a milking certificate in influencing the price Ot a cow, or her offspring, has ben a noteworthy feature of the Shorthorn sales of the past few years. High prices are not common for Ayrshires,'. but the first prize cow in the milk record class at Ayr, the stan- dard, eligibility for which is 800 gallons Milk of not less than 3.5 per cent. butterfat in the year, was bought by an American breeder for £2OO. Since Ayrshire breeders, have adopted the cus- tom their breed has gained new supporters in the Colonies and in foreign countries, and brought higher prices than were given before. - —" The Times."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

HINTS BY AN EXPERT.

One 'never needs to look far for something to do in the garden now, the showers of the last few clays having made things spring up vigor- ously where a short while ago all seemed dry and barren.

The frequent use of the hoe is of great ad- vantage between the rows of vegetables, as it checks the young weeds that are now beginning to make an appearance; it also keeps the sur- face from baking and lets light and air to the roots of the plants, besides disturbing innumer- able pests detrimental to the well-being of the plants. To those who are anxious to grow some good onions, I would advise them to sprinkle between the rows a few handfuls cif common salt; if this is done in time it will keep what is known as the onion maggot away, as well as stimulate the growth of the onion. An excellent way to prevent birds from eat- ing the peas when they have come up is terun some black cotton just about four inches above the ground the full length of the row in a zig- zag fashion. The birds on alighting strike their breasts against the cotton and it scares them away. This has been tried with marked suc- cess when all other remedies have failed. Soot, where it can be obtained, should be freely used at this time of the year. The recent rains have tended to set fruit on the trees, washing away the decaying bloom and with it the thousands of insects. Pears can now be said to be fairly safe. Keep the water- ing can out of the garden without it is abso- lutely necessary, as you are apt to give either too little or too much, and either is bad.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
MISCELLANEOUS POINTS.

Seeds of Pansies and Violas sown at once and kept in a cold frame until they have made nice plants, will bloom this year. When the, seedlings have made a few leaves they should be transplanted into other boxes and kept growing with the lights on, but tilted at the back for air. Start Dahlias into growth by covering the tubers with soil and giving a good watering. Stand the boxes in a cold frame. Plant out from frames Violas, Pansies, Pent- stemons, Snaps, Phloxes, and Calceolarias, ,if. they have been sufficiently hardened. Put a mulch of long manure over Strawber- ries, and give liquid manure. A sowing,of Kidney Beans may be made on a south border.

Xfter pruning Roses see that each tree is firm in the ground. If the tree is easily shaken press the soil with the foot firmly up to the tree stem.

The Golden Rods (Solidago) are distinct from all other yellow flowers, and are excellent com- panions of the Michaelmas Daisies during Sep- tember and October. Roots may he planted now. An uncommon and dainty annual is Fenzlia Dianthiflora, which forms a dense tuft of rosy flowers, with a yellow eye. It grows three to six

TAXATION & LAND VALUES

Examination and Criticism of son:e Recut Reports

IMMINENT LEGISLATION.

The Executive of the Land Values Group in the House of Commons has issued a survey, in which the Reports of the Departmental 'Corn- mittee on Local Taxation and of the Land Enquiry Committee (Urban) are examined and criticiied in_sp far as they deal with the policy of the Group on the Taxation and Rating of Land Values'.

As the document states: "Seven members of the Departmental Committee are opposed to rating on land values, while the remaining six support the proposal The Land Enquiry Com- mittee-are unanimously in favour of rating land values. Both Reports register the advance in public opinion which has taken place during the last decade, and show that legislation on the matter is now imminent" - , The survey constitutes "a concise add lucid summary and criticism of the recommendations for land values taxation contained in the two Reports, and a searching criticism of the objec- tions to this system advanced in the Depart- mental Committee's Report. It is a valuable piece of informing literature and places within a small compass the salient points dealing with the land values question, which, without this d, be available after a laborious sgtuuidye of fotuilid n e only two o1)11114 volumes just issued to ' the public. To political and economic students, therefore, the Survey should prove of great value. The United Committee for the Taxation of Land Values (11, Tothill-street, London, SW.) have, by .pernaission of the Executive of the Land Values Group, decided to issue the Survey as a supplenient to the May issue of (Id., published on 1st); it will Published in pamphlet form. phasises the fact that both the Ls:uaLTbnashdenegdiSurvey EnquiryeuVnratvlletyty ee Values" e: Committee and the separate Report of the Departmental Canmittee recom- mend a considerable transfer of rates (estim- ated at not less than ten millions) to site values —" a great gain that this recommendation, with the reasoning on which it is based, is now held to apply universally, instead of being restricted, as in the Separate Report of the Royal Commis- sion in 1902, to urban districts and to certain special rates." ' The Reports are criticised in so far as they "lack a frank avowal that the new system, in applying public values to public purposes, is in itself right and necessary as a matter of social justice. Nor is there any clear statement to be found as to the liberating effect of the change in oPPortnnities for labour and thereby enabling u abling it to secure its fair reward." This point is dealt with fully, and particular stress is laid on the case for "land value as a just measure of contribution." Strong criticism is levelled against the pro- posal to rate agricultural land at a lower land value rate than other land. "In the case of agriculturat holdings,' it is stated, "as in the case of all other land, those who enjoy the land value ought to contribute on the basis of that value, and the industry of agriculture, so far from having any fresh burden put on it, would be relieved by the transfer of the charge from the value of improvements on to Tie land value."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPERIAL AND ,LOCAL TAXATION.

With regard to the fransfer of certain charges from the local to the-imperial Exchequer, the Survey strongly re-asserts the Group's demand for a national land Values tax to provide the necessary revenue, because "this is the only way to give real relief from the rates which now press upon industry. If the burden 're- mains on the taxpayers without any national levy being made on land values, it will remain, only slightly disguised. as a burden on industry. The national levy will his° be fair and just for landowners inter se, because the tax will fall lightest. where land-Values are'l6west." The national tax is Ow:stated _to be .neeessaiy in order to prevent grants to relief of rates becom- ing, in reality, subsidies to landowners.

THE INDISPENSABLE The combination of rate and tax on land values is stated to be "the minimum which can be acceptable." :pre policy of .the Group is summarised as follows,:— • " The Land Values Policy is (1) putting 'on :a national basis part or the cost of some• of thee services which are now borne by local rates. If thia,were done by a grant, without any national tax being imposed on land, values, it would re- sult admittedly in a bonus to landowners. The method of doing it by a national tax on land values is acknowledged to be fully defensible on principle. It would cause no injustice, but would , distribute the existing burdens among the persons who enjoy land values in a way which would be fair inter se, and which would not impede and penalise development as the present system does. . Side by side with this reform, it is necessary (2) that a moderate direct burden should be; placed on land values to the relief of industry in the shape of. a land value rate, to pay for future ,increases in local ex- sal rate of Id. in the X, together p esni similar i rre u, na a, regards existing expenditure, a with an option to the local authorities to in- crease that rate in the ,C from time to time."

BUTTER MADE FROM TREES.

There are several trees that yield an oily sub- stance much resembling butter and making a good substitute for it. The Bassia butyracea, or the " shea butter, " as it is commonly called; grows ;wild on the west coast of Africa. The butter obtained from this tree is derived by pounding and pressing the seeds, which, are three inches long. The seeds of the crab tree of Brazil and Guiana are 70 per cent. fat. When boiled, the tallow turns out yellow butter. From the nutmeg a useful fat is obtained. A tree abounding in the forests of Africa and called by Kaffirs " chignite," yields a white butter-like substance, which constitutes an important article of commerce on that continent.

LAST AND FIRST. Curious Coincidence at Warrington Rural Council.

COUNCILLOR FURNESS AS CHAIRMAN

It was rather singular that Wednesday's meet- ing of the Warrington Rural District Council was the last of the old year and the first' Of the new year. Councillor Mather, late -chairman, mentioned that the last meeting sliPped away without any of them thinking about it. The Clerk took the chair at the outset.

Councillor Mather (ex-chairman) proposed, Councillor Ball seconded, and it was carried unanimously, that Councillor Furness be ap- pointed .chairman.

Councillor Furness said in reply that he would be glad if he could have the same support that had been accorded his predecessor. Councillor Mather .had made things run smoothly and had held the office with honour and dignity 'o the Council, and he (the speaker) proposed a vote of thanks to him for his services. He knew very few men who devoted so much time to public affairs in such a diversity of positions as Mr. Mather.

• Councillor Ball seconded,. and Councillor Longton, in supporting, said Councillor Mather had been an ornaruent, but a very useful one, and he wouLl :Ike to seelini fill the office again on some future occasion. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell, also supporting, said, although he had not always seen eye to eye with Councillor Mather, he had always appre- ciated his work. lie was one of the most dili- gent men with whom he the speaker) had come in contact. _

===== 0002601_19140509_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
UNIQUE EVENT.

Councillor Mather, replying, said it was unique in the history and annals of the Council that the appointment of chairman and a voi.e of thanks to the retiring chairman should come together. They had had no final meeting, for it slipped away before they thought of it. . It was unique to have the first and last niwcting within the half-hour. He proposed the oldest member of the Council, Mr. Longton, to be the vice-chairman.

Councillor Ball seconded, and said a more able man they wuuld never have when it came his turn to take the chair.

Councillor Furness said that Councillor Long- ton had qualified himself to occupy the position.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
-ROADS AND FOOTPATHS.

At a meeting of the same Committee the Clerk rread correspondence with the Clerk of the County Council arising out of a strong protest made against the unsatisfactory.' condition of the main roads within the Council's district, con- cluding with a promise of early attention. Further correspondence was read re the con- dition of the footpath from Hood-lane to Butter- milk Bridge to the effect that the agent for Lord Lilford would fall in with reasonable im- provements of the road. The committee, in view of the great advantage to the public, fav- ourably considered the proposal, the terms and alterations to be submitted to the Council, and, if carried out, the Parish Council to continue responsible for the future repair of the road.

PENKETH HOUSING. The Penketh Parish Council having desired to call the Counhil's attention "to the urgent necessity for more houses in Penketh," also asked "the Rural District Council to use the powers conferred upon them by the Town Plan- ning Act, and to erect houses in that township.' The Clerk was directed to inform the Parish. Council that on the information before theni the Council could not move in the matter. ELECTRICITY FOR SANKLy. A letter from the Borough Town Clerk stated receipt of applications for a supply of electricity from residents of Great Sankey and Penketh, and that the Corporation were willing to do so on the same terms as existed within the borough, but before approaching the Lancashire Electric Power Company for their consent, the Corporation would he glad to know whether the District Council would support their applies-

_ tion. The Clerk was instructed to reply in the' affirmative. The minutes of the General Purposes Com- mittee were confirmed.

TO "PIG IT" TOGETHER. Arising out of the Inspector's report as to certain houses, the Chairman said people were willing to "pig it" together anyhow to keep down expenses...

After giving his report, which was adopted, Mr. Newcombe, the surveyor, mentioned that the main roads were getting into such a poor condition that the heavy motor traffic was seek- ing out rural district roads. They should not, in his opinion, make their roads which ran oft the main roads too attractive, or the traffic would be tempted off the main road. A lot of drivers would go a long way round to get on District Council roads. Councillor Mather paid a tribute to the work done under the Surveyor, and instanced the roads at Poulton. NEW VICARAGE.

The plans were passed for the new Vicarage at Orford.

GARAGE ACCOMMODATION.

One of the principal obstacles to the wider popularity of the light car is undoubtedly the dearth of suitable garages, coupled With the proportionately high expense of this method of car housing. The modern suburban residence seldom has accommodation for any vehicle more ambitious than the motor cycle and sidecar, a fact which probably accounts for some of the prosperity of the lineal descendant of the safety bicycle. The average garage is far from satis- factory in many respects; the high cost has al- ready been alluded to, but what of the careless handling, frequent movings, scratched enamel, and even the risk of one's car being used for the purpose of joy rides? A leading provincial journal suggests a good way out of the difficulty ! —the co-operative garage. In most towns of any size there should be little difficulty in the way. The co-operative garage should be about the last word in cheapness, second only to the motor house at home; as profit would not be the end in view. Besides, each member would have a personal concern in the good manage- ment of the establishment.

CYCLING AND MOTORING. Road Hogs the Common Enemy of Mctorists as of Pedestrians THE CO-OPERATIVE GARAGE.

I have always thought it a pity that no better protection against bad weather has yet been provided than the waterproof cape. It seems to be unsatisfactory from almost every point of view. First of all, it is seldoni really water- proof, as most riders will beewilling to testify; then most of the water seems to find its way on to one's knees and to crown all, the average cape forms a perfect obstacle to wind. Apropos A this last point I remember hearing of a rider who met with death on Kirkstone Pass several years ago. He was riding along the precipitous road when a sudden gust of wind blew the cape over his head and before lie could recover him- self his machine was dashed over the rocks, with fatal consequences. The cycle cape is not 0601 and comfortable, as its votaries would make out, but quite the reverse. For all ordinary purposes I much prefer a short waterproof coat such as worn by motor cyclists. It may be rather warmer, but is certainly much more com- fortable.

The Care of Variable Gears.

Until the universal adoption of the free wheel the safety bicycle was a remarkably simple con- trivance, but in the mechanical world improve- ment often means complication, though some- times the reverse. The variable gear is a case in point. In one of the cases in the Science Museum, South Kensington, a typical three- speed gear is exhibited,- both in toto and dis- mantled. I had ridden one for some time with- out any trouble, but five minutes at South Ken- sington cenvinced me of the necessity for greater ' care in the future. The mechanism is very -delicate, immensely strong if properly used, but 'liable to resent carelesir treatment. • A cam-. Øi 4iionpoe of.krauble,:is the . stretching of the cable, which, if not actually putting the gear out of action, results in severe strain, as part of the cogs must do the work of the' whole. After all adjustment is quite an easy matter. Always free wheel when changing gear, if you want your hub to give the best service. In cases of abso- lute necessity it does no harm to pedal slowly, but it is just as_well to he on the safe side. the Bicycle in Italy.

•4won

• Great Britain always seems the home of the cycle as of the locomotive, but appreciation of its attractions is by no means limited to these shores.. According to the " Gazetta dello Sport" cycling is immensely popular in Italy. At the end of 1912 there were no less than a million riders in that country. Of this number the greater proportion is domiciled in the provinces of Ravenna, Venice and Emilia, the latter in- cluding many skilled artisans, enthusiastic cyclists like their English oonfreres. In other , provinces such as Sicily, Campania, Calabria and Molisa, the pastime is less popular. While the excellence of the British machine is ad- mitted on all sides, Italy is making a praise- worthy effort to build up a prosperous home industry, and already our own manufacturers who specialise in the Mediterranean trade are beginning to feel the pinch. Even an export trade of some magnitude is gradually being built up., In 1912, for example, 4,281 machines valued at £27,398 were exported from Italy, compared with 626, valued at .t 4,256 in 1908. On the other hand, imports have declined from 8,914 machines, worth £57,049, in 1911, to 5,435, valued at .635,784 in the succeeding year. Spring Lubrication. 1 It is only during the last year or two that any serious attention has been paid to this im- portant subject. After all, roads being what they are, good springing is indispensable, and good springing implies lubrication. I can well remember a ride in a farmer's cart through some of the rural lanes of Southern Germany. The road was past description,- and the cart , devoid of springs! But some automobiles are very little better, for the simple reason that springing is non est, owing to continued inat- tention. It is a comparatively simple matter to insert some good lubricant between the leaves and the improvement in running is most notice- able. The latest device in this connection hails from America, taking the form of a separate oil cushion to be inserted between the leaves. The cushion is simply a thin perforated strip I of steel with the holes filled with suitable lubricant and covered with a thin Membrane to facilitate transport. When in the spring the latter mixes with the lubricant, which retains its effectiveness for a very considerable time. Motorists and Road Hogs... •

Bearing in mind the extensive local knowledge possessed by every scout it is by no means unlikely that the efforts of the Associa- tion will be successful. Ever since the motor car has been of any importance, the different organisations have adopted a very commendable, attitude towards road hogs. As Mr. Joynson Hicks, M.P., the chairman of the A.A., said only the other day, "We are anxious to do every- thfng within our power to bring reckless motor- ists to justice. CHESHIRE REST HOUSES,-IL

BY SILVER-STREET. By an accident on Saturday I discovered two more houses worth noting. The W.W.C.C.'s run was to Davenham, but as one of their sensible rules is that a majority at the start have power to change the run if the wind opposes, the 26 set off to Cornhill Farm on the Chester-road at Mickle Trafford. It was half-past four when they found their lucky stars were not that day in the ascendant, as they so often have been this year. Mrs. Blackburne was not at home, and only seven could be accommodated. Ten. obtained an excellent repast at the Railway Inn at the top of Dunham Hill, where Mrs. Scowsill provided for so many at some incon- venience to herself. The remaining nine suc- ceeded in finding an equally favourable haven at the Shrewsbury Arms, just over the River Gowy.

Pedestrians wishing to visit Plemstall Church, or to explore the flat but delightful country round Ince, Elton, Iliipsford, Thornton le Moors and Wimbold's Trafford, will find the Railway Inn only a stone's throw from Dunham Hill Station, and any party up to five or six can be comfortably served for tea. Larger parties must go to Bridge Trafford, as mentioned above, or to Cornhill Farm, which I fully treated of last year.

THE KIRKSTONE PASS. J.N.W.—Yes, I know one person who has rid- den up the Kirkstone, although I am not sur- prised to read your own doubts as to whether .anyvile hairever.done it. It is "Kuklos,". the cycling editor. of the "Daily News," formerly of another good Radical paper, the "Yorkshire Daily Observer." It was on October 20th, 1910, and I never heard of anyone doing it before or since. I will give you his own account from my own file of interesting cycling bits that I have collected from many sources An empty ambition, you say, to ride a bicycle up Kirkstone Pass! Admitted, but I was a keen road-racer once, and shall never quite lose interest in a mere physical struggle against odds. Further, my friend, the Cuckoo, is a hard rider of the first class who believes, as I believe, that Kirkstone Pass will never be ridden on a bicycle with a single gear of normal ratio. Where we part company is my ancient gospel that variable speed hubs, when properly made. are a great mechanical advantage in hill- climbing. At Glenridding they were quite scep- tical that the deed was possible, though Mr. Milerest, who has lived at the foot of the Pass for 20 years, thought I might get within a quarter of a mile of the top. The morning was quite favourable to me, for the wind came briskly from the north. This, I thought, was a kindly and timely recognition of the fact that I do not blaspheme it—well, hardly ever—when it opposes me.

.. --- At the foot of the Pass, just below the lone tarn of Brothers Water, there is a finger-post which says "Kirkstone Top 31 miles." Here you have a fine view of that curly grey ribbon soaring away to the clouds. I made a pencilled note of the time, 11.50. In innocent over-confi- dence I had lighted my pipe, but it was soon put hastily away when I realised that I had possibly bitten offi in the Yankee phrase, more than I could chew. For the middle mile of the climb I did a good deal of " donkeying," or zig- zags, for which some of my critics refuse to ad- mit any advantage, and that only means that they do not know how to do it; but on the last stretch there did not seem to be width enough in the road. I could not be quite sure when I was on the steepest portion. If it is one in sir as usually claimed, there must be a mile at least of one in seven or eight. When I passed the notices at the top, and slipped in the nor- mal gear for the hundred yards to the inn, I had had quite enough. I dismounted at the Traveller's Rest at 12.18-28 minutes for the 3i miles. I had ridden up Kirkstone Pass without a dis- mount on a Pedersen cantilever bicycle geared to 56, 85, and 127, with Bin. cranks, and a fort- night's luggage aboard. The 56 gear was in for three miles. Some local shepherds and farm hands in the inn said I was the first to ride it and probably the last. They knew of one local rider who attempted for a wager of a sovereign, but failed. Anyone who doubts that I rode it has 6nly to send me a retvn railway ticket to Penrith, and I shall be pliased to do it again. If I took the same machine again I should want the handles lower and more forward, and I might reduce the normal gear to the usual 10 to 1 ratio. Had the Pedersen been thus equipped and with no luggage on, the job would have been very much less difficult. It may be admitted that the ride did me no ec appriable good. For the first time this year I Perspired heavily; I had all those distressing symptoms at the small of the back which ought to have meant my instant need of Some One's cramp in Kidney Pills; there was a touch'l° f trembled a one calf, and for, an hour or tw good way up. th . pThe surface of e roaod was good all the

THE CHANCELLOR'S MESSAGE .OF

HOPE. ' - Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S 1914 Budget has not created the storm of opposition that its forerunner, the 1909 Budget, did. The reason is that the Opposi- tion know that violence would be use- less. Both these great achievements, great as they are, may be looked upon as mere starting points. Just as the first Budget and Mr. ASQUITH'S prelim- inaries cleared the way for this week's move, so this week's advance is only preparatory to what is to come. As MT. PHILIP SNOWDEN declares, the amount of the national grant to muM- cipalities cannot be taken as anything but a beginning; and he infers that the call the CHANCELLOR has made upon the largest incomes is comparatively small now so that they can be held in reserve for a future occasion. Briefly, what Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has done this year has been to face a large prospective deficit, largely caused through the additional demands of the Navy, and while doing so he has taken care that the demands of. social reform shall not be neglected. To the deficit caused by armaments he has created a further deficit in favour of these reforms, and goes to work unconcernedly to gather in the necessary money for both. No , new taxes are sprung upon the country, but he goes to the super-tax payers and the richer income-tax payers for his new-needed millions. That seems only fair. It is clear that public expenditure must go up if we are to maintain our place in the world's race. We must have Dreadnoughts it seems; Mr. LLOYD GEORGE says we mu&have education, sanitation, town Planning, and rational housing for the masses, upon which the wealth of the nation ultimately depends. All agree Upon the need of the expenditure, but the parties join issue as to the objects upon which it is to be spent, and the persons who are to be called upon to pay. _

- this is where the difference of policy as between the Progressives and the Conservatives asserts itself. Conser- vatives do not object to any expenditure upon Dreadnoughts. They want eight and they won't wait. But when it comes to social reform and providing for the comforts and necessities of the poor, it is." extravagance," and we are sapping our reserves, which, they per:. versely pretend to believe, ought to remain intact for the time when we are at war with somebody .or other. It was because the Conservatives were expecting war and then went to war in South Africa that they could never see their way to providing money for Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S old-age pensions. Liberals have found that money and are leaving the wars to look after them- selves. The certain needs of the poor first; then the problematic needs of war. But are we extravagant? Even with expenditure topping the £200,000,000 point we are well within our means. Sir GEORGE Pstsx, editor of the " Statist " and one of the leading authorities on financial subjects, assures us that the burden of taxation, in spite of the great increase in expen- diture, is now much smaller than it has ever been in modern history. In 1850 the nation's expenditure was over 50 millions, but such at Budget was more oppressive on the people when the gross income of the country was only 500 millions, than is four times the \mount of expenditure with four times he amount of income. So that we can till sleep peaceably in our beds. On the question who shall pay there ,s as sharp a division of opinion be- ween the parties. The Conservative Teciflc is Tariff Reform that would :pread the payment over a large sur- ace by means of indirect taxes upon lur commodities. In other words, the Aothing, the shoes, and the food of the labourer would be taxed—out of all Iroportion with the same things bought )y the . rich, because of the latter's larger means—to make that labourer pay for his old-age pension and the other comforts to which he is entitled. LLOYDGEORGE have meneothuogdh,isantod mmaokree Thethose pay W ,han enough—and take their at und- :nce out of the community to which ,hey are but asked to pay a little in return. And to that message of justice to the people the Liberal party are committed.

COMMITTEE ON SMOKE. The Government have appointed a strong Departmental Committee "to consider the pre- sent state of law with regard to the pollution of the air by smoke and other noxious vapours, and its administration, and to advise what steps are desirable and practicable with a view to diminishing the evils still arising from such pollution." Time was when the reference of such a question to a Committee meant shelving it, but the present Government have shown that when they move they do so for practical pur- poses. ,Something, therefore, is likely to be done to make it possible to force manufacturers to try to keep clear the atmosphere of the filth they at present turn into it in the name of trade and industry. The character of the Committee proves that a serious attempt is to be made to grapple with the problem. Lord Newton, who knows this part of Lancashire well, is one of , the selected members; it is largely due to his advocacy that the Government have taken this forward step. Gentlemen well versed in the subject from Leeds, Sheffield, Wolverhampton, Manchester, Glasgow, and other centres, where the air is polluted, will join Lord Newton in the effort to level up the law on the subject. At the same time they may evolve a method that will ensure the administration of the law when it has been strengthened, for, as is well known, were the present laws properly carried out, there would be much less cause for com- plaint. Public opinion is quite prepared for ,action. a point upon which the Committee, no less • than the Government, will duly bear in mind.

ER, SATURDAY, MAY 9, 1914. pASSING WEEK. ILATE MR. J. BUNCH, Impressive Scene at the Interment at St. Paul's Church.

THE GREAT SANKEY THROSTLE. During the past winter and present spring a throstle has been a prominent figure in the every-day life of Great Sankey. The bird made its appearance nearly six months ago, and drew special attention to itself in consequence of its miserable appearance. It had only one leg. The other was defective and drawn up, either by reason of malfermation or a fracture. Its plumage was also deficient, it had no tail, and, altogether, it looked as though it had escaped,' as by a iriiracle,'lrom the jaws of a cat in the very moment that the cat's feast was ,to begin. Dishevelled, forlorn, and evidently outcast, the throstle claimed the sympathetic interest of a number of men working on some houses Mr. Furness is erecting near the station. The men, responding to the advances, fed the bird regularly, and it became tame and regular in habit. In the sprint; the throstle, though the use of the leg had nt been recovered, though the feathers were no more attractive, and thbugh the tail was still absent, PrOved that it had not lost its voice. Taking up, a position on the topmost branches of an ash tree overlooking the buildings, upon which its benefactors of the winter were still at work, the bird has sung as throstle never sang before. The fine calls, the full musical runs, and the pure whistle Which are the peculiar attributes of the thrush, and single him out for first favourite among our singing birds, were specially developed in this instance. The contrast between the quality of the song and the sad appearance of the singer was most marked, and was the subject of much interested Speculation.' Was the bird singing a special .hymn of praise for his winter's keep? Was he making a special effort to attract a mate? The mate came not, and to-day he is singing his best and loudest, solitary, and pro- bably downcast, but intent on looking on the world that has treated him so badly, with, at all events, an outward gaiety.

"A SCENE." For the second time in a very short period a Warrington- public body has had to be ad- journed because of a refusal of one member to obey the ruling of the chair on a matter of order. In the first instance it was the Educa- tion Committee that suffered this week it was the Town Council. We do not wish to go into the relative- merits of the dispute on either occasion, hut itAlUes,seem a pity that any one gentleman should take upon himself the respon- sibility of Sending home a whole public body composed of nearly -forty members, to manyoof whom time is valuable, before they have fairly begun the business they have specially met to transact. If single members find they have this Power of sending everything awry when the one elected to preside over a meeting decides that they are transgressing, where shall we end? A member, wishing to be away, yet anxious to have a voice in a particular matter, might raise a scene and defy the chair as a ready way of bringing about an adjournment. We must devise some other means by which the one shall go and the rest remain. The adjourned Council will take place on Wednesday.

THE RATE COLLECTION. In the-few minutes during which the Council sat time was found to pay a well-deserved com- pliment to the Assistant Overseer, Mr. W. 11. Woodcock, and his staff, for the uniformly splendid result of their collection of the various rates. During last year rates amounting to £134,095 were collected with a loss of only £l7 13s. Old. In collecting nearly a quarter of a million for Poor Bate during the last four years the total loss, arising from all causes, including bankruptcies and absconding persons, was only £25 18s. lid., or an average of £3 4s. 9d. per half-year. The amount excused by the magistrates on account of poverty was less than £4O. These are figures which speak elo- quently not only for the Collector's office, but for the industrial prosperity of the town and the moral integrity of its inhabitants.

OUR LE.A.RNED SOCIETIES. 'Warrington is welt stocked with learned Societies; in fact, those who try to keep pace With several of them find their task very diffi- cult. Not only do meetings occasionally clash, but subjects overlap and, perhaps worst of all, there is no central place in which to meet, and to which a member can turn safely without first carefully consulting his notice card. For several years the question has been discussed whether our societies could federate, provide themselves with a hall common to the whole, and draw up programmes on a co-ordinated basis that would prevent irritating clashing of 'dates and subjects. The proposal took definite shape this week, when representatives from the Warrington Society, the Literary and Philo- sophical Society, the Photographic Society, the Field Club, and .the Shakespearean Society, formed a joint committee to draw up a scheme which will be submitted to a future meeting, to which representatives of other societies are invited. Mr. H. Womersley, who was appointed honorary secretary pro tem., will be pleased to hear from anyone requiring further information than that he imparts in a letter in to-day's "Examiner."

RADIUM AND CANCER. We have every ,reason to believe that local medical men are extremely anxious that War- rington should join with Manchester in her task of raising £33,000 for a radium fund for experimental work in dealing with that dread disease, cancer. The seriousness of the fact that one woman in every Seven over 35 years of age, and that one man in every ten over 35 years of age, die of cancer, in one or other of ,its forms, is only exceeded IV the fact that at present science attacks the disease almost in vain. A medical correspondent in "The Times" says What would be of the greatest value would be a campaign of education whereby people might be taught to recognise the earliest symp- toms of the disease and so be able to seek treat- ment before the chance of sallation has passed. There can be no question that this view repre- sents the whole truth. Surgery is still the best treatment for cancer. Moreover, thanks to an advance in knowledge of the anatomical paths tbryeawtmhiecnht tthheandisieas gseenuesrautlill lyy. eeadosa,atnnci.e,.,(si, surgery curable met Taken hod of early and operated upon thoroughly, cancer is The great mischief is that the public do not ever-growing percentage of cases. know this. They regard surgery as a forlorn hope, and even when they suspect that they are the victims of the disease hide the suspicion and delay treatment In addition to his im- proved technique the surgeon has now at his disposal both radium and the X-rays, each of which have shown, in certain cases, very re- markable results. become eraveure-Ehromwore effective

It is to follow up these remarkable results, position, that radium funds are which promise to find a way out of the present unsatisfactory being, inaugurated in various Parts of the country. The Manchester fund is growing encouragingly, because the feeling so well ex- pressed this week by Sir William Mather, when sending a donation of £l,OOO, is generally understood. "It. appears to me," he wrote, "that the need is so pressing, and the success so promising, that sacrifices are called from all classes in some degree tb bring this benefi- cent remedy to the relief of all sufferers with the least possible delay." Places around Manchester may help in rais- ing the fund, and are promised assistance in return. Bury has opened its radium fund this week, the dim being to raise £2,000 to work in conjunction with Manchester. The advantages of co-operation are that the city has resources that will enable it to purchase a sufficient quantity of a very expensive commodity, and that it has its own special physicist, Sir Ernest Rutherford, who was awarded the "Noble" prize for research in radio-activity. If War- rington raised £2,000 it would be taking a worthy part in a war that must be waged against one of the greatest scourges of man- kind.

v. FRIENDS PAY LIST TRIBUTES.

The remains of the late Mr. John Bunch, who, as stated in last week's "Examiner," passed away at the Warrington Infirmary a week ago, after an operation for appendicitis, were in- terred in St. Paul's Churchyard on Monday afternoon. The large congregation which gath- ered to witness the sad ceremony bore eloquent tribute to the deep respect in which the de- ceased gentleman was held by the wide circle of his friends and acquaintances. _ _ .

_ Before leaving the church the Vicar, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, delivered a brief address, in the course of which he said that the present was the saddest funeral lie had witnessed during his twelve years' connection with the parish. It was impossible for them to understand the loss of a life so young, so exemplary—the life of one who was so clever in the highest sense of the term. He extended the sincere sympathy of the church to the father and family. They had gone through a dark cloud, and there must be some silver lining to it.

„ As the funeral procession passed out of the church towards the graveside, Mr. E. Wilkes played ” 0 rest in the Lord" and other suit- able musical selections upon the organ. Messrs. W. and J. Dutton were in charge of the funeral arrangements.

KILLED ON THE LINE.

Widow's Compensation Claim. A sequel to a railway fatality at Collins Green Station last December was heard at the War- rington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, when a claim under the Workmen's Compensation Act was made by Mrs. Florence Mary Adamson, Station Cottage, Collins Green, for £275 for the loss of her hus- band, who was killed on the L. and N.-W. Rail- way at Collins Green on December 23. For the plaintiff Mr. A. Browne, Warrington, said that the deceased was employed by the company as a platelayer, but occasionally, after finishing work, it was alleged that he had performed acts of service under the orders of the stationmaster at Collins Green. On the night of December 23, the deceased, it was alleged, was acting as a porter, at the request ' of the stationmaster, who gave him a lamp to send a train out of the station. On crossing the line he was knocked down by an empress goods train and killed. For the company, Mr. S. Pope, barrister, of Manchester, contended that the act of service after hours was not done under a contract of service with the company, because the station- master had no authority to employ him. The wages sheet would bear out the fact that when doing such work on the station deceased was not employed by the firm at that time. De- ceased received payment for work as a plate- layer only. The services which deceased ren- dered on the platform were entirely voluntary, and were given as acts of friendship to the sta- tionmaster.

Several witnesses were called on both sides, and at the close of the clay the hearing was ad- journed until. june 11th.

Amusing Experiences Pleasantly and Amusil Recounted.

His health having been enthusiastically drunk and his portrait having been presented to him, Mr. BOYS, who was greeted with cheers only second in enthusiasm to those accorded Mr. Law, caused roars of laughter by the humorous man- ner in which he dealt with himself and his "ample" proportions. It has been a great privilege, said Mr. Ryder Boys, to be invited by you to play the part of Falstaff. Of course there were very great diffi- culties in the way of my playing Falstaff—the difficnities were not confined to adequate pad- ding. (Laughter.) We had many suggestions— things to be blown up. (Laughter.) Balloons had gone out of favour and aeroplanes had takni their place. We telegraphed all over the country. Among others we telephoned to Richard Flanagan, esquire, an authority upon stuffing. (Laughter.) He sent back a most out- rageous message. "Of course," he said, " e shall be very pleased to make the stuffing—if you require it "—(loud laughter)—and after I had been undergoing treatment for twelve months. (Laughter.) Fortunately, I was with Mr. Law at the time in the Infirmary, and he . supported me as I clung to the machine and said something in the vulgar language that is attributed sometimes to Shakespeare. (Laugh- ter.) But these difficulties were overcome some- what. Then, however, came the momentous question: "Is there such a thing as a buck basket to be had?" (Laughter.) Mr. Law or Mr. Ashton, or the other Mr. Ashton, or some- body, said: "Leave that to us." They under- took that dreadful responsibility, and when I arrived at the night's performance I saw a great abyss. (Laughter.) There are many people I wish to thank for " supporting " me so loyally —(laughter)—those with whom I had to hold converse, those whose hands I kissed, and those whom I embraced—(laughter)—as well as I could. (Loud laughter.) I would like to thank those four gentlemen who supported ID3 most manfully and carried me away, to their very great delight. (Loud laughter.)
