SECRET OF SHAKESPEARE'S POWER.

Mr. LAW, proposing "The immortal memory of William Shakespeare," professed that he knew very little of a great subject. Ho felt he had, in a way, peeped into a very great treasure house replete with jewels of inestimable value. He had been privileged to glance at a few of them, and had recognised what wealth was left behind; and still remained to be dis- covered. Whatever the world might do to raise a monument to Shakespeare, none could be of greater value or last longer than his own works. (Applause.) It was strange to contemplate that Shakespeare wrote 300 years ago; that he was acknowledged in his day as a man of superlative merit; that now his fame is greater even than it was in his own day; and that he was the one author who was read throughout the whole world by men of every nation, sz3 cry colour, and every creed. Racine and Moller° made their special appeal to Frenchmen, Schiller was known to all Germans, and Confucius to the Chinese, but there was no man except Shakes- peare whose work was known by the people of every nation. Why was it that this man, of whom we knew so little, who had a humble origin at Stratford, went to London, and after some struggles became an author and an actor, retired to Stratford, died, and was buried in Trinity Church—how was it that the work of that man had such an extraordinary hold upon the whole world?

Miss ADAMS responded and welcomed ladies who had appeared with the col:LILO the first time this year. One of the chief, tages of participation in the commemorati that it gave them a much better know the plays than they could otherwise gai° plause.) _ ,

The Poet of Nature. Ile thought the reason was this: feelings of: Old as the n world is, the natural instincts _ man have not altered. Man to-day, in spite of his civilisation and the alteration of his environ- ment, is exactly the same creature as in the time of the ancient Egyptians, ten and twenty athreoutsahnedsaymeaer7 ago he 6 loves, Hises, hates,feelin revengesandpassiaonnds strives as his ancestors did long ages ago. Shakespeare is the only man in the world who has ever been able to represent man as he is, irrespective of the age in which he lives, his occupation and his surroundings. It was be- cause we knew ourselves and loved ourselves that we loved Shakespeare. "Hamlet" was possibly the most popular play ever written— because every man, although he might not con- fess it, found in the character of Hamlet that there was something of himself. That strange quality was in all Shakespeare's works; and Shakespeare was liked because he held up the mirror to Nature, and because in his works every man was able to see something of himself. (Applause.) There was little likelihood that Shakespeare would ever be forgotten or become unpopular. His popularity was, in fact, increas- ing, and all sections of the community bowed. in reverence before him. Not the actor alone loved his Shakespeare—every man was able to read Shakespeare and enjoy him. The Bible was the only book that was more widely read. That being so, Mr. Law continued, we must all feel a pride in being able, even in the humblest capacity, to associate ourselves with the memory of a man who lived, not for an age, but for all time, a man who had a vocabu- lary so great that it exceeded by five times that possessed by the ordinary well-educated man. The knowledge of Shakespeire was tremendous. There is nothing that has ever been done to which Shakespeare does not, in some way, make reference. There is no feeling which he does not express, no human passion which he does not fittingly represent. The lawyer, the doctor, the parson, the scholar, the artisan—each one finds something in Shakespeare that appeals to him. This being so, I think that we gathered here to-night may pride ourselves upon our being privileged, in some slight measure, to be associated with a genius who has never been surpassed, and, I venture to say, never will be I surpassed. (Applause.)

Life and Death.

The births in Warrington last week numbered 47, giving a rate of 33.3, compared with 35.1 in the same week last year. The deaths totalled 20, giving a rate of 13.8, compared with 16.1 last year. Forthcoming Property Sale. Messrs. John Mainwaring and Co., instructed by Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., announce a sale on 27th May of valuable property in Bridge- street, Wilson-Patten-street, Dolman's-lane, and Lower 13ank-street.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Barnabas' Church.

A sale of work will be held in the Patten Hall on Wednesday and Thursday. The Mayor opens the first clay's sale, and Mr. Harrison will perform the ceremony on Thursday.

A Wedding Gift. The members of the Orford-lane Junior Con- servative Club presented, on Monday, a silver rose bowl to Mrs. bolt Waring, who was married to Captain Waring the previous week. As Miss If. Parr she was a prominent worker in con- nection with the club.

Mayor at Catholic Banquet. The Mayor (Councillor P. Peacock) was pre- sent and spoke at the WallaSey Roman Catholic Banquet on Wednesday. There was a distin- guished company, including the Bishop of. Shrewsbury, the Lord Mayor of Manchester, and the Mayors of Wallasey and Bootle, and Mr. Owen Took, formerly of Warrington.

Alderman as Pile-driver. Rapid progress is now being made with the construction of the new Warrington Bridge. The last of the 430 permanent concrete piles, which have been driven in the walls and abut- ments of the bridge, was driven on Thursday afternoon. Aldeiman Bennett, who happened to be present, gave the last blow and completed the job.

May Queen Honoured. As it was the first time in the history of the Warrington Band of Hope that one of the mem- bers of the British Women'a Temperance Asso- ciation had been crowned May Queen, Mrs. Simpson, at a meeting of the local branch of the association, presented the " Queen," Miss Eva Tanner, with a gold pendant set with sapphires and pearls to mark the event.

Nature Study Rambles. The Adult Schools have arranged Nature study and antiquarian rambles for the summer. The first takes place to I3ewsey Hall to-day, when Mr. Charles 'Madeley will be conductor. Other dates are: 13th June, Hill Chile and Appleton (Mr. G. A. Dunlop); 11th July, Halton Castle; 15th August, Grappenhall Reyes; 12th Septem- ber, Warrington Parish Church; 10th October, Old Quay Canal.

A Warrinutonian's Success. Mr. Charles Horobin, son of Mr. H. R. Iloro- bin, 51, Sankey-street, has been appointed Chief Water Engineer to the City of Lincoln. Edu- cated at the People's College, he served his apprenticeship under Mr. Deas, the then War- rington Water Engineer, and under Mr. Gray was engaged in the installing of the Winwick supply. He became Assistant Engineer at Ipswich, and three years later was appointed to a similar position at Lincoln, where he now becomes chief.

Cycle Motorists' Outings. Warrington Motor Cycle Club visited Chester on Saturday, and spent part of the time in a motor boat upon tlie River Dee. On Sunday they were at Little liudworth, and met th€ Liverpool Club. Speed testing was clinic, through, Mr. Phillpotts, secretary of the Anti Cycle Union driving past the Warrington an Liverpool men, who had to estimate the spee4 at which he was driving. The Warringtoi men were the winners in the estimating test.

Wesleyan Synod Meetings. We call the attention of our readers to the series of public meetings and religious services to be held next week in connection with the visit of the Liverpool District Synod of the Wes- leyan Church to Warrington. We may say it is a rare thing for an ecclesiastical body of such importance to hold its sessions in the town, and it is something of an honour that this year we should have the Synod in our midst. The pub- lic meetings held in connection with it have all of them a splendid platform, and there is a treat in store for all who can attend. For particulars of meetings see our advertisement columns. -

School Boy's Sad Death. Much regret has been occasioned to the teachers and scholars of Arpley-street Schools during the past week by the somewhat sudden death on Wednesday morning of one of the scholars, George Sankey. The death was due to laryngitis, brought on by the boy's remaining in the Warrington Baths for over an hour on Friday night. Deceased, whose age Was nearly twelve years, was the son of Mr. George M. Sankey, of Monks, Hall and Co., and lived at 240, Liverpool-road. At school a member of the , 6th standard, he was exceedingly popular amongst the teachers and scholars. He was an enthusiastic swimmer, and for three years had taken prominent parts in the school's annual concerts with great success. At the time of his death Sankey was studying for the Technical Institute Entrance Examination, his school- master looking forward with confidence to his success. Dr. Naden had charge of the patient, With whose parents the greatest sympathy is felt. The funeral will take place at the War- rington Cemetery at 2 p.m. on Sunday.

Funeral of Late Mr. W. Jones. The remains of the late Mr. William Jones, Green-street, were interred in the Warrington Cemetery on Sunday afternoon, in the presence of a large number of sorrowing relatives and friends. At the graveside the last rites were conducted by the Rector of Warrington (Rev. Canon Willis). The coffin, which was of polished oak, had brass fittings, and bore the following inscription: "William Jones, died 28th May, 1914; aged 85." Gathered round the grave, ' amongst others, were: Mr. T. Wadsworth, Mr. P. Peers, Mr. T. Power, Mr. R. Whitby, Mr. F. White, Mr. A. Tilling, Mr. S. Dunbobbin, Sze. Floral tributes were contributed by various friends and members of the family, including Mr. and Mrs. John W. Jones, "Owen and family," "Alfred and family," "Fred and Sarah," Mr. and Mrs. Lee, Mr. and Mrs. B. Stott, Mr. and Mrs. W, Jones, "Jim, John, Fred" (grandsons), Mr. and Mrs. F. White, Misses Whitehouse, Sze. The bearers were Messrs. F. Mills, T. Waring, A. Foster, W. Rookes, R. Bloomfield, and F. Bennion. Mr. W. Stephens, Green-street, had charge of the funeral arrangements.

Drank Poison in Mistake.

Mistaking a bottle of nitric acid for brandy, Richard Newton, rag gatherer (58), 3, Dial- yard, Warrington, drank a quantity of the poison and died in the Infirmary on Wednes- day.—Coroner Brighouse conducted the inquest yesterday evening, when it was stated that dur- ing the wife's absence deceased drank the poison and on her return she asked him why he had taken the poison, and he replied "I don't know."—" Death from misadventure" was the verdict returned.

Warringtonians at the Royal Academy. This year Warrington is exceptionally well represented at the Royal Academy. Sir Luke Fiides exhibits three portraits—Dr. W. Bullock, Mrs. Romer Williams, and Mr. A. E. Barton. Mr. Henry Woods has six small Venetian sub- jects—" A Riva on the Grand Canal," "Statue of Bartolommeo Colleoni," "A Farm, Loggia, Veneto," " Fondementa San Sabastiano," and "A Neighbour." Mr. Oswald Garside has two water-colours—" Solitude" and "Moret-on-the Seine "—and also has exhibits hung at the Paris Salon and Royal Institute of Painters in Water-colours. Mr. Stanley Pierpoint shows a painting, "Field high is the freshet." Miss R. M. Dakin has a miniature, "Fairy pictures," and Messrs. W. and S. Owen show architectural designs of Lady Lever Memorial and Christ Church, Port Sunlight.

LADY GUARDIAN. Mrs. Simpson About to Depart from Warrington, A USEFUL CAREER.

The many friends of Mrs. Simpson, a member of the Board of Guardians, and an active tem- perance and religions worker, will learn with regret that in consequence of her husband re- tiring from business, she is. about to sever, her long connection with Warrington. Mrs. Simpson has had a most interesting career, and her services will be greatly missed. For 11 years she has been secretary of the local branch of •the British Women's Temperance Association. She has been a member of the National Executive of that body for seven years; and had the honour of being a guest at Castle Howard when the Temperance Women were entertained by Rosaland, Countess of Carlisle. She is also a vice-president of the Warrington Band of Hope Union and a member of the Wycliffe Tent of Rechabites. For some 12 years she has been a Sunday school worker in con- nection with the Buckley-street Mission, and is president of the Bewsey Women's Adult School. In public life Mrs. Simpson has played a noble ; part. She has for seven years rendered valu-1 able work on the Board of Guardians and its committees, and has served on the local National Health Insurance Committee since it came into operation. A PRESENTATION.

At the British Women's Temperance Associa- tion meeting on Wednesday, Mrs. Owen an- nounced that Mrs. Simpson was about to leave Warrington, and in recognition of the good work done she presented Mrs. Simpson with a handsome handbag of Rutland Russia leather, with gold fittings, and bearing Mrs. Simpson 's initials. Other members spoke of the great in- fluence which Mrs. Simpson's life had had upon the town.

Mrs. Simpson feelingly expressed her thanks for the gift, which she would always value as a token of their love for her. She felt the sever- ance keenly, but she hoped to see them from time to time.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
WITHOUT A LICENCE.

Important Prosecution in Warrington. 4 -

A singular case came before the borough magistrates on Tuesday, when George and Mary Ann Wilcock, 4, Selby-street, were summoned for acting as pawnbrokers without having a licence.

_ Mr. _ Pearce Mellor, solicitors' department, Customs and Excise, London, prosecuted, and Mr. A. Browne appeared for the defendants. - After explaining the particular sections in the Act, Mr. Mellor said that goods were left at the defendants' on the understanding that they could be redeemed. They had to protect those traders who were licensed Pawnbrokers, .and who should have the monopoly. In the present case interest at the fate of 2d. in the is. was charged. After stating instances where the act of pawning had been committed, he said Mrs. Wilcock had given a statement to the police, in which she admitted the offence, stating that she was a second-hand clothes dealer. She sold the goods back for a reasonable profit. No witnesses were called, and Mr. Browne said the case was similar to a large number of large number of other places in the town called "putty shops." The parties had believed they were doing nothing wrong, as no exception was taken to the practice when they informed Supt. Williamson and Detective-Sergeant Reddington what they were doing five years ago. They had acted foolishly or under a mistaken impres- sion of what they were told. The Chief Constable said the defendants were cautioned in 1909.

On the parties giving an undertaking to give up that particular part of the business, they were let off on payment of £.3 7s. costs.

Pardonable Grouchiness. Captain Robert C. Warr, about to retire from sea life after 49 years of it, said on the Cam- pania: " Yes, it is true that sea captains are some- times annoyed by passengers who think they know more about navigation than the navi- gator himself. "I know a captain to whom a passenger once said : " 'What town is this we are approaching, " Derwent, sir.' "'No, cap., you are mistaken. Look at this ' map here. According to this map it's Fordham- on-Tyrie: "The captain said nothing, and a moment later the passenger asked : " What channel is that, captain?' '"Egg channel, sir.' " Why, man, you're wrong again! The map gives it as 11tellin's channel.' "Three or four times this sort of thing went on. Then the passenger, pointing to a gull, said : "'What kind of a gull is that, cap.?' " ' Look at your map and find out,' the cap- tain gruffly answered."

TENNIS, CRICKET, CROQUET, and other out-door Sports.—J. S. GARLAND, Complete Sports Out- fitter, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.

National Finance goes hand hi hand with effort for National Well-being.

THE RICH TO HELP THE POOH.

Mr. Lloyd George, in his Budget this year, has accomplished an achievement as great as when he produced his epoch-niaking Budget of 1909, with its resounding effect upon that entrenched position of wealth and privilege, the House of Lords. Besides being a financier, Mr. Lloyd for the amelioration of the people on hand he ; and when he has schemes George is a reformer also has the power of the purse to back up his ambition. It has been objected that this dual character of Chancellor of the Exchequer and reformer is an unsatisfactory arrangement, but we as a nation are coming to look upon social reforms as investments that bring us in manifold profits- Appreciating that fact more and more, we increasingly Jejoice in the possession of a Chancellor who sees in the money he handles something more than the metal we call gold. liVhen the imagination of Mr. Lloyd George has permeated the country— land than it is. as it is slowly doing—England will be a happier

school days. Warrington has taken powers under the Act to feed its necessitous school chil- dren, but trade has been so good that it has only had to be brought into operation on very rare occasions. These increased powers will be found very_ useful.

There will he grants for physical training and open-air schools for tuberculous children. Among other things there will be:— An epidemic grant to cover the loss of grant'l caused by infectious disease, from which War- rington is a very heavy sufferer. Schools for mothers. To provide for heavy sickness among married women.

To help Deposit contributors. To relieve cases of arrears due to unemploy- ment.

•For provision of clinics. For medical referees to aid and advise the panel doctors. Health lectures.

There are also the general health and housing services, abolition of slums, provision of allot- ments, roads, and police. The rebate of income tax for children of men in receipt of the smaller incomes is increased from .klO to £2O in respect of each child under sixteen years of age. Thus a man in receipt of £2OO a year and having two children will be exempt from income tax.

Government Relief for the Over-burdened Ratepayers.

those with the income of £B,OOO and over pay lis. 4d.; in other words, these will pay double income tax.

The death duties. The present duties are in- creased from X 60,000 one per cent., and rise to a maximum of 20 per cent.; this instead of 15 per cent.

An Equitable Arrangement. This alteration is wise and equitable. No one can contend that any man can by his own abilities, his services to his fellows, amass, say, a million pounds. The accumulation of such wealth is due to many circumstances, for which the individual can take no credit. These death duties are useful instruments for redressing those glaring inequalities of fortune which are not only undesirable, but which are a positive danger, especially when wealth passes by in- heritance into the hands of those who have not the ability nor wisdom to use it well.

A STUPENDOUS BUDGET.

We have reached the stage of the £200,000,000 Budget. The Chancellor, on the basis of exist- ing taxation, anticipates a revenue of £200,655,000 in the new financial year. As the Estimates of expenditure aggregate £205,985,000, of .C. 5,300,000 to be made Chancellor of the Exche- quertl good.iere •s,d nothing to accentuate the diffi- culty with which he had to deal, but Mr. Lloyd osAun a would do dredfio icril eneYy George, as in .1909, seizes the opportunity to "ratify his desire for social reform. By his help to the local authorities and his proposals for feeding school children and quickening zeal for public health and education, the deficit is swollen from five millions odd to £9,550,000. By calling upon the Sinking Fund for £1 000,000—a legitimate act for a Government that has paid off .C 104,000,000 of National Debt— the Chancellor reduces the amount of new revenue he has to find to £8,550,000.

Those Who Are Able to Pay Find the Money. Every penny of this is to come from the com- fortable and the wealthy classes—in short, from income tax, super tax, and estate duties. What he proposes includes measures against evasion of income tax, and similar devices, all of which merely tend to make those who, under the pre- sent system, bear less than their share of the pressure of national burdens, pay what Mr. Chamberlain, in a phrase that Mr. Lloyd George would probably shrink from, "ransom" for the security of their vast property and interests. The amount would be larger if the increased taxation were not accompanied by concessions in which the rich man shares. In the briefest possible way the new taxation and accompanying concessions may be set out as follows New Taxation. Earned incomes over .£l,ooo—increased duty on graduated scale. Unearned incomes—is. 4d. in X, instead of Is. 2d. Super-tax begins (with new graduation) at £3,000, instead of .4:5,000. Death Duties on estates above £60,000 in- creased. Settled Estate Duty raised to ordinary rate. Income on capital abroad, if re-invested, to pay tax at Is. Id.

MILLIONS FOR Bottom's.

How Warrington Bates May Benefit by 9d. in the

EDUCATION AND HEALTH.

- - The most notable feature of the Budget is that the first practical step is taken towards making national objects a national charge. In the Chancellor's words "there has hardly been a year for the last forty years that successive Parliaments have not cast new functions of an onerous and costly character upon the local authorities, and very rarely with any provision to assist them to meet the financial liability." That is now to be rectified. Warrington was among the places Dir. Lloyd George mentioned as places where the rates had increased and slums remained. The rates in some districts have doubled in the last 20 or 30 years, he said. West Ham's rates have gone up from ss. to 10s. 6(1. since 1890; Merthyr Tydvil from ss. 3d. to 10s. 2d.; fNroolrr scBl.lsdfr.otmo 7785..1i1d1d. Amid all the pressure for reform, he declared,. to 10s. 6d.; Warrington "there is one undertaking which cannot be post- poned without injury to the interests of the nation, and that is the re-adjustment of the relations between local and Imperial taxation." (Ministerial cheers.)

THE NEW GRANTS.

Mr. Lloyd George's speech makes it evident that the Government have taken full advantage of the report of the Departmental Committee sitting for the last two years on local taxation, which was referred to several times by the Chancellor as the Kemp Report. This Com- mittee sat to inquire into the changes which have taken place in the relations between Imperial and local taxation since the report of the Royal Commission on Local Taxation in George 1901, with a view to the introduction of legisla- tion at an early date. The recommendations ._summmarised thus of the Kemp Committee, which Mr. Lloyd .efnotlal orwy-e dE, e various grants will be simplified into one block grant of 365. per child in average attendance, plus 40 per cent. of the net expenditure, minus the produce of a sevenpenny rate, tinder this arrangement the Government would pay Warrington £4OO of the d mayucat bo ne .€l,OOO if teachers' salaries were increased in any year by that amount. Higher Education.—The grant, which is now recognised as the residue grant, will be distri- buted proportionately to the net expenditure in Place of the method fixed in 1894. This will mean an increase for those districts which are doing their duty, and Warrington will consequently benefit. The grants will make technical and higher education more accessible to the poor; and give special training and Pensions for teachers in secondary schools. • Poor Law Relief.—The report provides for in- creased grant in aid of cost of maintenance of patients in asylums and imbeciles under the care of Poor Law Authorities. ===== 0002601_19140509_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE MONEY FOR INSURANCE.

The public health services cost the rates £15,000,000 a. year and the Exchequer under £200,000. Mr. Lloyd George proposes to make a public health grant equal to 25 per cent, of the net expenditure, and to use the contributions out of it to press local authorities to deal more spiritedly with housing, clearing of slum areas. provision of sufficient hospitals, tuberculosis, and provision of small holdings. Urgency grants are proposed to secure— Tuberculosis arrangements for dependents of insured persons. Supply of trained nurses. Laboratories for scientific diagnosis. Besides these, it is proposed to spend A:1,250,000 in connection with the Insurance Act in making grants to well-managed societies abnormally hard hit, helping deposit contribu- tors, organising health lectures, the establishrnent of clinics, and the appointment of medical referees not only as a check on malingering, but for consultations in serious cases;

AMOUNT OF RELIEF.

The national allocation of grants in aid of Local taxation for the full year will be : England and Wales £9,200,000 Scotland Ireland

Total

This will be equivalent to relieving the rates by 9d. in the X, but some authorities will get more and some less. On the existing basis of rating St. Helens will benefit by just under 9d. in the X, while Walsall will benefit to the extent of is. 2d. The Chancellor did not men- tion Warrington in this connection, but it is reasonable to assume that the amount of relief would not be less than the amount of relief assigned to St. Helens.

REVOLUTION IN RATING. Magnificent Courage of the Govern. ment and Chancellor.

TO REDRESS INEQUALITIES.

- - --- Mr. Charles E. Parker writes:— Whatever critics may say about introducing . such a complicated Budget a session already so congested, all must admire the magnificent courage of the Government, and especially of the Chancellor. Whatever be the inconveni- ences—the probability of an autumn session and e increasing like—to Members of Parliament, no one can deny that a revision of the burdens of rates and downta xes has been broeetnestr may have been somewhat an- reasonable overdue. The cry of "keep reasonable in view of the benefits the ratepayers enjoyed; at the same time this was good ground for the contention that local authorities have of late years been compelled to provide funds for purposes which were of a national character, and further that every authority has been obliged to provide propor- tionate funds when their resources were alto- gether disproportionate:. Mr. Lloyd George gave some striking illustrations of this. Ife instanced Bournemouth, where a penny rate raised as much per child in the elementary schools as a sixpenny rate in Walsall.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Application.

So much i i abi t se p given, resent indefinite about the Budgett that it is impossible to say w say, in the case of our what helpu ic) ro w p o sl sl a local rates. We know, however, that the sum set aside for this purpose for Great Britain and Ireland is over four millions for insurance, edu- cation and local grants. I think, however, it will be a mistake to en- tertain the idea that this will result in much reduction, if any, in rates as a whole, or par- ticularly in local rates. The intention prim- arily appears to be to give such help as will enable local authorities to develop methods and machinery for the accomplishment of necessary reforms in regard to health, sanitation, housing, education, road and street making, which the local authorities, however willing and ready to undertake, are at present unable to tackle, because it is considered unfair and unwise fur- ther to increase local burdens. No doubt some relative relief will be enjoyed by those who are rated on their improvements, from the fact that, in future, site values are to be made to contri- bute and to be made an integral and independ- ent source of local taxation. Altogether the Budget implies a revolution in rating. It suggests the possibilities of far- reaching benefits to the ratepayers. These will be under the control of local authorities, but the control authority is to exercise a super- vision, and the amount of some of the grants will depend upon how they are used locally. ===== 0002601_19140509_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION RATE BURDEN. Councillor Ilenshall Rejoices at the Coining Adjustment for ‘Varrington.

INSPIRATION TO REFORMERS.

• " Hope deferred maketh the heart sick," characteristically remarked Councillor Hen- shall, when interviewed by "The Examiner" representative. "It is a long lane that has no turning, for it is 18 years since Lord Balfour of Burleigh Committee began to inquire into the relations between Imperial and local taxation, and it sat for five years before issuing its re- port. Although the Budget of 1909 promised some relief, the present Budget provides the first practical contribution to the relief of the growing and almost unbearable municipal bur- dens."

It was, he said, most refreshing to hear the Chancellor of the Exchequer saying in the House of Commons what had been said for many years in the Council Chambers of the country. Councillor lienshall said it was of special local interest that what had been contended for several years in relation to the Government sub- ventions for purposes .of education was conceded. Proposed further relief which was anticipated would ultimately reach an amount equal to 9d. in the 4 on the rates, which would adjust the anomaly in Warrington.

How the Local Burden Grew.

In 1905 the total expenditure for education in Warrington was £37,550, of which the National Exchequer contributed £27,142 and the rates £10,408, but practically every year since then the proportion contributed by the Exchequer had been less, and the difference had had to be made up from the rates. The estimated cost ' for the present year was £51,056, of which £30,056 would come from the Exchequer and £21,000 from the rates, whereas if the propor- tion of 1905 had been maintained the Exchequer would have contributed £37,856 and the rates £16,200, or, in other words, the education rate would have been Bd. in the less for the cur- rent year and the estimated difference for 1915 would be exactly the 9d. in the 1, which the Exchequer relief is ultimately expected to yield. Councillor Henshall said he thought that municipalities had much cause for thankfulness in this year's Budget, and that its provisiont for secondary education, feeding of necessitous school children, and the recognition of the duty of the State, not only to impose responsibilities, but to assist in bearing them, would be a great encouragement, and, in fact, an inspiration to all civic reformers.

VETERAN TRADESMAN DEAD.

Demise of Mr. Thomas Grime.

By the death of Mr. Thomas Grime, of Clif- ton, Helsby, at the ripe old age of 85, Warring- ton has lost not only a respected figure, but also its veteran tradesman. For the past two years Mr. Grime's health had been gradually declining, but ten days before his death a serious change in his condition was observed, and he sank rapidly. Mr. Grime was a native of North Lancashire, and first took up his residence in Walrington on his marriage with the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Carter, of Warrington about sixty years ago. It was then that he commenced the drapery business in Bridge-street, which has been so long connected with his name. He did not retire from active service until he had passed his 80th year, and then his retirement was deeply regretted in trade circles. Until about 40 years ago Mr. Grime resided in a picturesque old house at the end of Slutcher's-lane, Warrington; he then removed to Thelwall, and 20 years afterwards went to Helsby, where he lived until his dehth. Mr. Grime was a prominent Mason, and had passed through all the offices of the "Lodge of Lights" during his connection. A few years ago he was the recipient of county honours. He was also associated with the Bold-street Wes- leyan Church; at Thelwall and Ifelsby he be- came a member of the parish churches. The deceased gentleman, whose wife died in 1900, , leaves three daughters to mourn his loss.

THE FUNERAL. The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday afternoon. There was a service in llelsby Church earlier in the day, ' and was conducted in an impressive manner by ' the Revs. E. W. Evans and J. Blakemore. The principal mourners were Mrs. Greening and Mrs. J. F. Dutton (daughters), the Misses Greening and R. Dutton (granddaughters), Messrs. J. F. Dutton (son-in-law), J. Greening (grandson), J. Bate, Berkers, Wood, Percy Sil- cock, Meadows, W. Collinson, S. Collinson, Bagnell, W. Grime,- Percy Carter, and Dr. Bryant. The following ladies and gentlemen also at- tended at the Cemetery: Miss Robinson, Messrs. N. Greening, L. Greening, Walter Dut- ton, W. T. Lee, llesketh, Bailey, 11. Clarke, A. Taylor, C. E. Bunch, Greenough, llignett, Leigh, James Carter and Stanley Carter. Messrs. W. Roberts and T. Proud represented the War- rington Tradesmen's Association.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

STRONG HIPPODROME PROGRAMME.

A host of good turns are to be seen at the Hippodrome this week, and whilst for the last fortnight or so we have seen many " first visit" turns, some of the old favourites pay a welcome return visit and merit the round upon round of applause which they get Tom E. Hughes, the rag-bag of vanity, sends the audience into con- vulsions as the " Aristocrat," whose acquaint- ances are many and varied. He has some good comedy "business," and is a hot favourite. In a " confidential " manner Robb Wilton treats the audience to some good things, notably his burlesque on the policeman, which is full of wit and humour. Described as the penny air- men, the Kenna Bros. introduce smart gymnastic feats in a humorous but clever fashion.- S. W. Wyndham is the essence of comicality, whose laughter-provoking propensities are decidedly novel. On the suspended cable The Gilberts do some daring tricks with such amazing rapidity and dexterity. In a well-modulated tenor voice Reginald Crawford delights the audience with renditions of well-chosen songs, such as " The Trumpeter." Freda Danilo is a perfect male impersonator with a captivating style, and Queenie May also scores a success as a character vocalist.

Next Week's Programme. The managers of the Hippodrome present a splendid programme for next week, which in- cludes John P. Traynor and Co. in "The Devil's Elbow"; Susie Delmore and Four Gentlemen; Will Athlone and Cissie Sinclaire (the drawing_ room speciality artistes); Tom Lee, in mono- logue; Josie De,laine, a versatile little lady; Polly and Day, in " The Crazy Waltz; the Three Macjahns, in comedy bar act; and Mdlle. Doria and her dogs in "The Maid's Absence."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Her Mother to Blame." In this new drama, set in India, there are many thrilling yet novel incidents, which are presented in an excellent manner by a capable company of artistes. "The Death Ride" at the close is full of excitement, and "Selah," the horse, is the centre of attraction as it gallops on the moving track. Miss Eveline Kington as Rosemary Frobisher, from Burmah Hills, is a delightful and talented actress, whilst the other important parts are in the able hands of Mr. Gerald Byrne, Mr. Richard Wagner, and Mr. Clarence Ibbotson.

A Strong Play Next Week. " Her Night of Temptation," the drama which will form the attraction next week at the Royal Court Theatre, would be just as good under any other name, if, perhaps, not so alluring to the general crowd. The fact is that it is an exceed- ingly good play, well written, well thought obt, and deals with a subject of even greater interest than Mormonism, or the White Slave Traffic. One of the many up-to-date features of the play is the introduction of a full-sized British mono- plane, in which the hero sets out to save his sweetheart from dishonour. The scenery and music are exceptionally beautiful.

LOCAL SOCIETIES.

Scheme to Federate and to Provide Suitable Hall.

Toth. editor of "The Examiner." Sir,—On Wednesday evening last a meeting of secretaries of local societies was held in the Photographic Society's rooms at the Old Academy. Alderman Bennett presided, and the following societies were represented: Warrington Society, Literary and Philosophical Society, - Photographic Society, Field Club, Shakespearean Society.

The meeting was called by the Warrington Field Club, to consider the advisability of local societies federating together, for purposes of co-ordinating and stimulating their activities. by various means, such as preventing overlap- ping of dates, and perhaps most important, the provision of a permanent and suitably equipped meeting place. In a brief introduction I stated how the Field Club, along with other local societies, had found that such a scheme was almost a necessity; an that due credit for this idea must be given to Alderman Bennett, who mentioned it, in a paper before the Literary and Philosophical Society'. in 1905.

meeting

As this preliminary meeting unanimously ap- proved of the principle of the Association of Societies for the purposes above mentioned, it was decided to form a Joint Committee repre- sentative of our local intellectual associations, to draw up a scheme of federation, to be put later before the societies themselves, to be adopted, if approved. A meeting of this committee will be an- nounced later, and all our societies will be asked to send their secretaries.

In the meantime the following scheme of federation was drawn up:—

1. To form a federation of societies in- terested in Art, Literature, Science and Philosophy, or any allied subject. 2. That a council be formed, consisting of two members from each constituent society. 3. That the object of federation be to co- ordinate and stimulate the activities of various societies interested in all or any of these subjects. 4. Each society to contribute to the ex- penses of the General Federation per member.

5. To provide, if possible, a common meeting place, with suitable equipment. Perhaps it would be as well if I point out a little more clearly the ideas we have before 'us in organising this federation. In the first place, the scheme, as outlined, is not to be considered cut and dried, but as just that necessary to start such a movement, which will, later, be submitted to the societies for approval and 'amendment. Although a town of very large population, it is surprising that, unlike a large number of other towns, Warrington has noth- ing of the sort that we suggest. There is, too, a very great lack of interest in the various small societies, and it is felt that such is accounted for by two main reasons. In the first place, societies overlap both in objects and meetings, and in the second Voce, there is a very great instability given to our societies by the want of suitable permanent meeting places. It is the overcoming of these troubles at which we aim. It is not in the least intended to do away with the individuality of each society, but, by conferring between the various societies, to prevent programmes from clashing, and, if thought fit, the publishing of a combined sylla- bus of the meetings for the session. This would probably be one of the first things that would be possible, would be possible in time to raise a As to the meeting place, it is thoughtbuthiladtinigt flecundtu,reasn,d,kbun,ildwiathsucituambmleuptleeace foouriusmeetings,whie h oe, eu lhda übiee These are the ideas we have before on nt hielilriinoeubss of the us, moderna n d I somewhat Institutes would ask all societies who a to the this movement to send a representative They re interested in me will all be written to, but it may be that so next meeting, to be announced later. ley 11 ' be inadvertently overlooked. I shall be pleased to give any further in- formation on request. Hon. Secretary pro tem. 20, White-street, Warrington,

ANTI-HOME RULE FARCE.

AMUSING INCIDENTS AT A WARRINGTON

TORY MEETING.

Quite remarkable were the events at a Con- servative meeting on Monday night at Cornhill, for although the advertisement styled it an "Anti-Home Rule" gathering, the speaker, a Mr. Brotherton, who was announced as coming from "London," did not make any reference to the question during his speech, and blamed the ' weather for it!

The Chairman (Mr. J. 11. Allen) said: "We are

hereto-night to continue our campaign against Home Rule," and after telling a ,heartrending .Story about boycotting by a number of Nation- alists who refused to have the conveniences of the telephone because a building necessary for the work was owned by a Unionist, he left the meeting. Mr. Brotherton, the "Anti-Home Rule" speaker, gave an address upon former Budgets • introduced by Mr. Lloyd George. He said he could not touch upon the Budget speech that was delivered that day, for he had not read it! This gentleman continued his Budget perform- . ance by "guessing at figures" con- tained in the newspapers which some of the audience were reading, and he is to be complimented upon his interesting item, which was somewhat spoiled by a slight political flavour. He assured the gathering that they could ask as many questions as they liked when the time arrived, and he went on to say that nobody liked taxes! Then he took his coat off—apparently he was warming to his work. He referred at length to the time when the Conserva- tives were in power and compared the cost of running the country with the present expendi- ture. Then he took his hat off. The extra tax

which has been placed upon tobacco and whisky came in for the, scorn of the speaker, who, ac- cording to his declared intention of dealing with interrupters, ignored the question " What about old-age pensions?" - He touched " Marconis," switched on a chorus of protests, and seemed relieved when he had turned them off again. Reference was made to a " brass " penny, which brought forth laughter, and the query " Have you seen one?' brought more laughter. Rain began to fall and the speaker invited questions.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEAKER HECKLED.

This was immediately accepted, when a young fellow asked " Can Ireland govern itself?" " I will answer that in the words of men who know better than I do," came the reply. "They are the archbishops, who belong to the same party as the Nationalists; they said 'No,' and I agree with them."

This led to the further question, "In 1885 a Lord said that of the lesser evils give Ireland Home Rule in preference to local government. The following year local government was granted, with the result that rates in Ireland were raised by threepence, whilst for the same period they went up 10d. in this country. If Ireland could do that thirty years ago, cannot they do that now?"

Air. Brotherton replied by asking "If they can do it now, why are the Dublin rates us, in the .t, whilst those of Belfast are ie.?" to which the young man retorted, "Are there no places in England where the rates are as high? You Ulster men don't want to come in and pay your share, you don't want to come in and help."

NOT TO BE DICTATED TO. There were numerowr interruptions, which caused the speaker to remark, "I am running the show and I am going to run it my own way I never allow anybody to dictate to me." (A Voice: "You are proper Tory-like," and laugh- ter.) •

_ , The young man in the audience was about to ask another question, when the speaker noticed lie held a book, and amid jeering he cried out " If*,,you want' to ask some questions, for heaven's sake put your book in your pocket! put your book in your pocket! never mind reading your questions." Then a voice was heard: "A lot of us at this meeting helped to return Mr. Smith at the last election, but we won't do it again." The rain was still falling and Mr. Brother. ton, although the crowd seemed anxious to stay, said he was not going to remain one minute longer in that kind of weather, and whilst numerous questions were being asked, he stepped down from the lorry.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 9, 1914.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
•

STOCKTON B EATH

TOWNSHIP TUTLE-TATILE.

The"gardeners have rejoiced. Rain came as a boon and blessing. Five degrees of frost also came. It blackened early potatoes and peas. And did damage to the fruit. trees. Thus it was not all boon and blessing. A modern Robin Hood was discovered By our energetic Police Inspector. This " Robin " said he stole not from The poor, but the rich was different. He will rest in prison a month. "Dad." quoth a young " Heathen " to his parent: "I have just seen Queen Elizabeth !" NOnsense„ she has been dead for centuries." "Well," exclaimed the youngster," "I have just "Seen Queen Elizabeth go down the Canal!" Then the rogue . explained thAt it was a ship so named. • The tramp with a legal knowledge is rare. One, however, made his appearance in The Stockton Heath Police Coprt this week And kept arguing with the magistrates. Ile rests now in prison, despite his Threat to appeal to the Home Secretary. Churchgoers from Warrington have found the Sunday morning tram service very incon- venient. The 10.15 a.m. "up express" got to Warrington Just in time to make them late for the ser- vices. Representations have been made to the Tram- way Department, And to-morrow the 10.15 will leave at 10.10 a.m. And continue to do so on future Sundays.

On the Bench.—The magistrates at the Stock- ton Heath Police Court on Tuesday afternoon were Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair) and Mr. C. Jackson 'Holmes.

Chairman's Holiday.—Councillor Mclntyre, the chairman of the Stockton Heath Parish Council, along with Mrs. Mclntyre, is on holi- day at Buxton.

Going to the Races.—Scores of horse and motor char-a-bancs and motor cars passed through the district during the three days of the Chester Races, and the politemen on point duty had a busy time.

, Tramway Breakdown.—ln consequence of the fusion of an overhead wire, a breakdown occurred on the Stockton Heath tramway route early on Thursday morning. The service was suspended for about half an hour, and much inconvenience was caused to the residents of the Heath who go to. business in Warrington, a large number having to walk. Fortunately no one was injured by the breakdown.

Cabman Fined.—At the Stocktcrn Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Joseph Rowne, cab driver, of Dolman's-lane, Warrington, was fined 10s. for being drunk in charge of a horse and han- som cab in Thelwall New-road that morning.

A Second Robin Hood.—At the Stockton Heath. .Petty Sessions on Tuesday afternoon, John -Murphy, of no settled abode, was charged on remand with loitering with intent to commit a felony on April 27.—Inspector Breese said he saw the prisoner loitering about in London- road. He saw him go to the rear of Mr. Cald- well's shop, and later followed him up London- road to the back of Mr. Turton's house. When witness inquired what prisoner was doing, he received the reply, "I want a pair of boots. I never rob the poor—only the Tich.'!—Prisoner said he only wanted an odd boot, and after a long list of convictions were read, he was sen- tenced to a month's hard labour.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
A POINT OF LAW. Tramp's Amusing Defence in Police Court.

When John Dixon, an "out of work," .of the wandering type, was charged on remand at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday with begging, he exclaimed in a loud voice, " Not guilty on a point of law." 'Prisoner took up a lot of the time of the Court. He had a long written statement, which he read, and in the most lawyer-like manlier cross-examined the witnesses.

Sergt. Wilding said on April 29 the prisoner called at his house. A daughter answered the knock, and witness overheard prisoner ask for bread. Witness put on his coat and hat and took the man into custody. Prisoner said he called it begging for alms when he asked for money; he was only asking for bread. _ . .

Prisoner: Did I apply for your daughter to be brought here?—You Has she been brought ?—No; she would _have been no assistance.

-- Who answered my knock?—My daughter. Then it was your daughter I asked for food, and not you?—Yes. What evidence have you got to prove your statement?—l have none.

PRISONER AND CHAIRMAN. That is my point of law. Doesn't the law of England state you cannot arrest a man unless you see him ask for bread? The Chairman (Councillor Roberts) inter- rupted, stating the Sergeant could not answer "points of law." Prisoner: I see; I see. He then asked the officer if he had given false evidence?—No. The Chairman again. spoke to prisoner, who replied, "You are not giving me fair play." The Chairman: You must not say that. Prisoner: I beg your pardon if I have offended you. Dixon then began to go into his "points of law" again, and was stopped by the Chairman. Prisoner: I see no other option than that I will have to make a statement. Inspector Breese gave evidence of charging prisoner with the offence. Prisoner: Gentlemen, I see no other course open to me than to go into the box and make my statement on oath. During the course of his long wandering statement, prisoner said he admitted asking the officer's daughter for bread, "but that was not evidence, because she was not present." The prisoner was wandering away from the subject of the charge, when the Chairman stopped him. " Prisoner: I shall have to petition the Home Secretary. Supt. Owen: What is your proper name?—l cannot tell you that. The Superintendent mentioned numerous offences, which the prisoner did not deny, and when asked if he remembered what name he gave for one offence he replied with prompti- tude, "I do, sir, John Wilson." The last con- viction was at Oakmere on March 2nd, 1914, when he was sent to prison for seven days for begging. ' Prisoner: I was convicted. I was innocent of 1 the charge. I can only petition; I can see noth- ing further for it. I will have to petition the Home Secretary. He was sent to prison for a month with hard labour.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
APPLETON.

The Riflemen.—There was a good attendance of members at the meeting of the Appleton Rifle Club, held on Saturday, when Lieut.-Col. Charles Lyon, its new president, fired the first shot of the season. Speeches of welcome to the new President were delivered by Messrs. Arthur Down and A. Shepherd, which were suitably replied to. - The club has gratefully accepted from Colonel Lyon a challenge trophy for com- petition amongst the members. During the afternoon the fourth match in the " Military Mail" League was shot with South Lurgan, Ireland. The Appleton score was 761, and the individual scores, were as follows: F. Tole, 99; A. Shepherd, 97; J. Hartley, 97; C. P. Day, 95: F. Black, 95; F. J. Groeott, 93; I. Broady, 93; F. C. Bennion, 92.

GRAPPENH ALL.

COUNCIL :1N1) SIIIP CANAL.

A Quest ion of a Sewer.

At the meeting of Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, Mr. Ashton, the clerk, read a letter from Messrs. Dunlop, Wattes and Co. re the piping of a ditch near Thelwall-lane, Grappenhall, and said that was the same open sewer that was referred to at the Lacal Govern- ment Board inquiry respecting the alteration of the boundary. Some months sate the. District ICouncil passed a resolution that the sewer be piped, subject to the sanction of the Ship Canal Company,-as it was chiefly Their syphon. They had written to the effect that they would in pre- pared to allow. the closing in of the sewer, pro- viding the District Council would maintain it as -a sewer. After the inquiry he (the Clerk) wrote to them pointing out that when the sewer- age scheme on the south side of the canal was carried out the ditch would no longer be re- quired by the District Council; and he asked them if they would agree to the Council main- taining it so long as they used it. The Company replied that they had no objec- tion to the proposal, but said they could not be responsible for the repairs to the drain under the road, as that was public property, but they would be responsible for repairs to the pipe in the ditch after the Council had finished- their sewerage scheme. The drain did really belong to the Rural District Council. The offer of the Company was accepted.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
METHODIST BAZAAR.

Intlependepts! Endeavour to Raise £3OO

for Building Fund.

A PRETTY SPECTACLE. a

The prettiest spectacle perhaps ever seen in Grappenliall is to be witnessed until to-night in the Independent Methodist New *Assembly Rooms, where a most effective bazaar is in pro- gress. The building is tastefully decorated, and the stalls are most daintily arranged. The bazaar started on Thursday, and ends this even- ing, when it is hoped the sum øf £3OO, required for the building fund, will be raised. On, the opening day the Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph) presided, and the Mayor (Councillor Peacock) performed the opening ceremony. Amongst those present were Mrs. Arnold Nield, the Misses Wilkinson, Alderman James Evans, Mr. W. Pierpoint, Councillor Henshall, Messrs. T. Critchley, T. Hamblett, R. B. Woods, A. Sanderson, J. Hudson, R. Nelson and E. Hough. Councillor Henshall _briefly " introduced " Councillor Joseph, which he described was like "taking salt to Lymm." (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19140509_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT WOULD HAPPEN?

The Chairman said he often asked what would happen if bazaars and sales of work were abolished? It was a serious question, and had a, serious aspect to the administrative side of church work, especially with regard to the financial department. If such an eventuality did come to pass, it would be a serious matter in more ways than one—not only from the financial point of view, but from the outlook of the church workers, whose efforts for the church must be stimulated by all working for one cause in friendly rivalry. Sales of work had not only a temporary good effect, but a-temporary effect in helping the particular church to throw throughout the dis- trict its Christian influence.

The, Mayor said that sales of work were places of trade and business, where they tried to get as much Money as- possible, but there was one little difference—they had no trade -disputes, no strikes. (Laughter.) He wished the ven- ture every success. Alderman James Evans proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and the Mayor.

YESTERDAY'S CEREMONY.

The opening - —••— • ceremony yesterday was per- formed by Mrs. P. Rylands, of Thelwall, and Councillor IL Roberts presided. On the -plat- form were Councillor Henshall, the Rev. J. S. Chedburn, Mrs. Henry Roberts, Miss Morris and Mr. A. Mounfield. • The Chairman said that was one of those happy occasions when all sects forgot their dif- ferences and co-operated for the common good. He hoped the Independent Methodists had adopted all the good points of independency. With Methodism he had no close acquaintance, but he knew that they had as a denomination given all they could for the common good in the past. Ife was glad that Mrs. Rylands was maintaining the traditions of the Rylands' family,

Mrs. Rylands said she was always glad to help good works that had for their object the spread of religion. (Hear, hear.) It was a good thing the various sects were able to join on common ground. In declaring the bazaar open she wished them all success. . _

Miss Hilda Ri-re-1;-;;;;ented Mrs. Rylands with a bouquet, and made a neat speech. Miss Mary Gough handed one to Mrs. Roberts, and Master Edward Howard gave a button-hole to the Chairman.

Mr. A. Mounfield proposed, and Mr. W. H. Bradford, of ,Grappenhkll, seconded, a vote of thanks to Mrs. Rylands and the Chairman. The total takings for the first day amounted to £95 3s. W.

WIN WICK.

Concert.—A very, successful concert was held in Winwick Schoolroom on Saturday evening. There was a large and appreolative audience. A good programme had been arranged, consisting of glees and songs, &c., by the choir, and Miss Parr, of St. Helens,. Mr. R. Mellin, Mr. Sweet- love, &c., and a ventriloquial entertainment by "Dick Danvers" and his assistant, "Joe."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
CANON PENRHYN HONOURED.

His 62 Years' Service in the Ministry.

FATHER OF THE DIOCESE.

Canon Penrhyn, rector of Winwick, was the subject of a singular presentation on Monday at the Church House, Liverpool, to mark his 62 years' service in the ministry. The genial Canon is the " father " of the Liverpool Diocese., The prgsentation took the form of a silver wg3oo;b4W, and a check.,?, and was intended Lis a token of personal esteem and affection for Canon Penrhyn, who has been a clerical worker in the diocese for fifty-eight sears. - The Bishop (Dr. Chavasse), who was in the chair, said Canon 'Penrhyn was one of their clergy whom the whole diocese delighted to honour. When it was known that at the ad- vanced age of eighty-five 116 had been called upon by the Board of Education to raise .21,200 to improve his schools, many of his friends felt that it was a fitting opportunity to show in some tangible form the reverence and affection with which they regarded him. Reverence and Affection. That reverence and affection were due to several causes, first to his age. He was the father of the diocese; he had faithfully worked for sixty-two years in the ministry, and for fifty-eight years in the diocese of Liverpool. He was finishing his course at Eton before he (the Bishop) was born, and he took his degree at Balliol Colltge, Oxford, when he (the Bishop) was three years old. They honoured him, too, for the invaluable services he had rendered to the Church. For eleven years he had worked at Bickerstaffe; for twenty-one at Huyton; and in all for twenty-six years as curate and rector at Winwick. In each parish he was still remembered and loved, for he was the true friend and spiritua guide'of his people, alluring to brighter worlds and leading the way. For twenty-two years he was one of their proctors in Convocation; for l thirty-four years he had acted as one of the hon.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
canons, and was now the last of that band of good men who formed the first chapter of the Liverpool diocese, when it was founded in 1880. But far more do they reverence and love him for what he was.

To them he had always seemed the very pat- tern of a Christian English gentleman; a sample of what the Church of England clergy had been at their best, and of what it was hoped they would be for many a day. A familiar figure at their chief diocesan meetings, firm in his own convictions, but with wide-hearted charity to- wards those who differed from him, he had lived so as to be loved, and they prayed God still to spare hire to them for many days to come, and, like the palm tree, to bring forth his best fruit in his- old age. (Hear, hear.) The Ven. Archdeacon Spooner, who, with the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson (Warrington) had organised -the testimonial, endorsed the remarks of the Bishop.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OCTOGENARIAN.

. The Bishop then presented to Canon Penrhyn the silver rose bowl and a cheque for £ll4. The money will be applied towards the sum of £1,200 mentioned by the Bishop for the im- provement of the schools at Winwick. The Rev. Canon Penrhyn, in acknowledging the gifts, said he would be eighty-six years old on Wednesday. A few weeks ago he celebrated the anniversary of his wedding—an anniversary which was more than a golden wedding celebra- tion. (Hear, hear.) On the proposition of the Bishop, thanks were tendered to the Wen. Archdeacon Spooner and Canon Morley Stevenson for 'their efforts as organisers of the testimonial.

PADGATE.

Late Vicar's Will.—The will of the Rev. G. W. Dickinson, who died at Padgate Vicarage a few weeks ago, having been formerly successively curate of Walton-on-,the-Hill, Liverpool; War- rington; St. Oswald's, Chester; Holy Trinity, Southport; and Vicar of Padgate from 1908, has been proved at .0,882.

. Wesley Guild.—The monthly devotional meet- ing was held on Monday evening, when an in- teresting paper was read by the Rev. H. Middle- wood on "David Livingstone." Mr. F. Wright presided, in the unavoidable absence -of the Rev. E. Morgan. Miss Ward submitted an ex- cellent report of the annual Guild Conference, held at Easter in Birmingham, to which she was the Padgate delegate. The roll of members was called by Mr. Harold Tomlin, and a mis- sionary hymn was sympathetically rendered as a solo by Miss Mason.

FEARNHEAD.

Farm Labourer's Suicide.—Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at the Rope and Anchor, Wool- ston, Warrington, on Tuesday morning, on .Thomas Ward, farm labourer, who was formerly in the employ of Mr. Herbert Houghton, Has- lam Farm, Woolston. It was stated that the deceased had worked for Mr. Houghton off and on for about eight years. Owing to some differ- ence with his employer, lie was dismissed a few days ago. Mr. Houghton stated that he paid deceased his wages on Friday night, and he re- fused to turn out on Saturday morning. He was a good workman, but he had had to dis- charge him several times on account of his drunken habits, and that was the reason for his dismissal on Friday. P.C.. Kaye said he met the deceased on Saturday night, returning to the farm. lie told witness he had chucked his job, as he was, "fed up," and he was going to leave the district. Next morning deceased was found hanging • from a beam in a hay shed at the farm. A verdict of "Suicide" was re- turned, but there was no, evidence lo show the state of his mind. '

===== 0002601_19140509_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANs.

A Light Agenda.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presiding. On the 'recommendation of the Children's Home Committee, it was decided to get an esti- mate for.the provision of a wooden rail to pro- tect the Palings. Also that applicants for chil- dren from the home shall, in future, fill in a form giving details of the home and the wages they wish to pay. It was decided that Mrs. Bowen, wife of Dr. Bowen, be appointed on the Ladies' Visiting Committee. •

===== 0002601_19140509_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM.

LINES FROM LYMNI.

They were not wedding bells. They were welcome bells that Rang so merrily a peal on Sunday. This was Lymm's ancient method Of giving a welcome to the Churchwardens. The fate of the Old Mill still remains. . At a point of considerable doubt. r, Some say it will become a palace. That is, a place where pictures will flash. ,Others think it will be pulled down To remove the danger at the corner. What will its final fate be? The simple life is not encouraged at Lymm. At least five tramps-who slept out Are decidedly under that impression. They were discovered by the police While taking a night air snooze. • • Now they sleep within the'prison walls. The police are very diligent in their efforts To suppress the tramp plague and the resi- dents.. are grateful. S.

"The Examiner was the only paper• on Saturday To announce to the gopd people of Lymm - That there was to be no May Queen. The traders regret the dropping of the old custom. Can it not be revived next t ear?

Ringing In the Wardens.—On Sunday noon the bells in the Lymm Parish Church tower rang out merrily to welcome the new wardens. '

Station Alterations.—The alterations at the Lymm Railway Station are now in full swing. The platforms are being raised by an additional 15 inches.

Licence Transferred.—An off beer licence at Rush Green, Lymm, was transferred from Emily Benton to Sidney George Collins by the consent of the Altrincham magistrates sitting on Monday.

Gas Extension.—The Burford-lane Bridge was closed to traffic last Sunday, when the Lymm Council's workmen were laying the gas pipes underneath in connection with the extension to Biooni Edge.

Postman for 39 Years.—Mr. T. Knowles, who has retired, has been a postman for 39 years. On Saturday he was presented with a handsome pipe and tobacco pouch by Mr. W. Arnell, post- master, on behalf of the Lymin Post Office staff. Mr. Knowles suitably responded.

Heatley Salt Works.—New borings are being made at the works at Heatley of Messrs. Verdin Cook and Co., Ltd. The premises are extending every week, and a new railway siding has been built. . A good quantity of salt is daily sent away.

New Phrase Coined.—ln his opening remarks at a sale of work at Grappenhall on Thursday afternoon, Councillor R. Henshall, of Warring- ton, localised the phrase " taking coals to New- castle" by making it read "taking salt to Lymm."

War on Sleepers-out Continued.—The Lymm Police are still diligent in their endeavours to suppress the sleeping-out nuisance, and five offenders were charged before Mr. Davies on Monday-, ,Four of the men were sent to prison for seven^days and the other was sentenced to 14 days.

Primitive Methodist Preachers.—ln announc- ing their recommendations of stations for min- isters for 1914-15, the Manchester District Prim- itive Methodist Synod, which sat at Hasling- den on Monday, recommended for Lymm and Altrincham the Revs. Robert W. Ferguson and William Daw.

The Old MM.—Despite the operations carried on at the Old Will at the foot of Eagle Brow last week, there still remains a quantity of machinery to be blasted out. The large water wheel will be removed and .the passage under the road from the Dam overflow to the wheel is to be built up.

• Cookery Classes.—The Lynim Sub-committee for Education propose to establish cookery and dressmaking classes, provided a sufficient num- ber of students enrol. It is often said that the young girls are deficient in these branches of domestic economy, and thus a chance is given them to improve their knowledge.

Tradesmen and the May Queen.—A meeting of the Lymm Tradesmen's Association was held on Tuesday night, when regret was expressed by the members at the abandonment of the May Queen Festival, as exclusively announced in "The Examiner." A suggestion of a children's sports was made, but nothing of a definite char- acter was, agreed .upon.

Church Council.—.A meeting was held in Lymm on Thursday evening to elect two repre- sentatives for the Diocesan Conference, and also to elect the Church Council. Messrs. Haylock and H. Pearson were elected the representatives, whilst the Church Council was re-elected as follows :—Messrs. W. G. Lecoinber, IL Oliver, A. Watkins, J.P., and P. W. Druce.

Cricket.—Lymm lost the first match of the season, which was played at home on Saturday, when Earlestonn 2nd were the visitors. The scores were 86-60, and the highest scorer of the day was Mr. Slack, a new player for Lymm, who made 19. To-day Withinshaw will provide the opposition to the first team at Lymm, whilst the second eleven are due to play Warrington St. Paul's, at Warrington.

Picture Palace, or What?—There are many rumours afloat in Lymra regarding the future of the Old Mill. It is stated in some quarters that a picture palace will be established there, but some local men, in consultation with "The Examiner" representative, state it would be well to have the building down altogether, and so minimise the danger of what is a dangerous corner.

Primitive Methodist Concert.—The recent tea party and concert promoted by the Broomedge Primitive Methodists was attended with great success. Forty people participated in an ex- cellent tea, but that number was considerably augmented for the concert, which was given by the Altrincham Primitive Methodist Prize Choir, under the conductorship of Mr. J. A. gill. Mr. A. J. Adams presided, and a pleasing incident occurred when Mr. and Mrs. Champ were thanked for their gift of the handsome suite of crockery presented to the church.

PASTOR'S SON'S DEATH.

Impressive Funeral'of Master Brigg.

Many tokens of respect and sympathy were witnessed at the funeral on Wednesday of Master Fawcett Edwin Brigg, the ten-year-old son of the Rev. and Mrs. H. 0. Brigg, now resident in Lymm, and lately of Rhodesia, South Africa. The little fellow, who had been suffer- ing acutely from an incurable disease since Christmas, was born in Rhodesia, and had only been in England since July. He had received attention at the Middlesex Hospital, London, where the radium treatment was of no avail, and had also received attention from Dr: be Jong, of Lymm. The boy's condi- tion became so serious that in February his father, a missionary in South Africa, was cabled for, and he immediately made preplarations for coming over. The lad passed away on Monday. Whilst in Lymm the deceased had attended the Lymm Grammar School.

- There was a very large gathering at the Lymni Wesleyan Church, where the first part of the funeral service Was held. It was conducted by the Rev. Jan Gunnell, of Penketh, who also officiated at the graveside in the Parish Church- yard.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
MOURNERS AND FLORAL TRIBUTES.

The principal mourners were: Rev. and Mrs. H. 0. Brigg (father and mother), Masters Brigg (younger brothers), Mr, and Mrs. H. S. Brown, Lymm; Mrs. Collins, Carlisle; and Mr. Fawcett Collins, Egremont. Wreaths were sent by the following: Two younger brothers, Lymm Wesleyan Sunday School, Mr. and Mrs. Finch`, Mrs. Cook, Miss Bell, Mr. and Mrs. E. Brown, Mr. and Mrs. H. S. Brown, Mrs. Pkwlyn, Mrs. Read (London), Mrs. Atkinson (Sheffield), Mrs. Collins (Cocker- mouth), grandmother; Mr. and Mrs. Dan Brown (Liverpool).

RECTOR TO WED.

Forthcoming 'Marriage to a London

Lady-.

DRAMATIC ANNOUNCEMENT.

The Rector of Lymin (the Rev. Cl. B. Thurston) is to enter the matrimonial ranks at an early date. The announcement of his en- gagement, which occasioned a pleasing surprise, was made in a dramatic manner at the close of a meeting to elect members of the Church Council held on Thursday night, and the parishioners are delighted at the prospect of the Rectory being occupied by a lady, who will be a great help-meet to the genial Rector in his many parochial duties. The bride-elect' is Miss Dora Evelyn Ransome, of Gray's Inn, London, and Holme Wood, Ips- wich. She is -a daughter of the late Mr. James Edward Ransome, for a long time a magistrate of Ipswich. Her brother, Mr. Edward C. Ran- Some, an ex-Mayor of Ipswich,'Nvas a friend of the bridegroom at Pembroke College, Cam- bridge. DATE OF THE WEDDING.

" The Examiner" representative was inforMed yesterday afternoon that the marriage will take place quietly in Londqn, probably in the second week in June. The Rector and the future Mrs. Thurston will return to Lymni, from the honey- moon, towards the end of June.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST ANNOUNCEMENT.

Towards the close of Thursday's meeting the Rector said he had an important statement to make, and then he quietly announced his forth- Coming marriage, and stated that he had been long acquainted with the lady's brother. He and her brother had been at College together. After the Rector had made his announcement congratulations were showered upon him. . _ .

Mr. A. Watkin was the first to 4rise. He spoke in kindly terms of the Rector, and said how pleased he was to hear the news. He wished them both long life and happiness. (Applause.) Other members spoke in similar terms, and these were Messrs. Callender, W. M. Beckett, F. E. Evans, and Dr. Burrowes.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
MAY QUEEN FAILURE.

Views of Local Tradesmen.

Yesterday " The Examiner" representative interviewed a number of tradesmen on the question of the abandonment of the May Queen Festival, as exclusively announced in the last issue of this paper. Generally much regret was expressed at the abandoning of the ancient custom.

_ "The festival," said one prominent trades- man, "should have been made purely a village function. A local band should have been en- gaged,-and this could have been done at much less cost, and the spectators would have been given quite as good a show. There should have been no Hippodrome kind of show, but one that would appeal for its beauty." Others expressed the hope that the scheme would be revived in the future.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE COUNCIL ASPIRANTS. A Stiff Contest in Heatley \Valli. MR. F. INGHAM AS INDEPENDENT CANDIDATE.

There are three candidates nominated for the vacancy in the representation of the Heatley. Ward on the Lymm District Council, rendered vacant by the resignation of Mr. S. Davies. Nominations were closed on Thursday noon, when the following nominations were handed in to Mr. Mallard, the returning officer:— Mr. .Frederick Ingham, butcher, of Heatley. Mr. William Finch, Woodlea, Lymm. Mr. James Jolley, of Pepper-street. NO POLITICS CANDIDATE.

Mr. Ingham, who stands as an independent candidate, is an upholder of the common-sense view that polities should not figure in a Council election. He is nominated by Mr. S. Gould, and he is seconded by Mr. A. Cowsill, which is sufficient guarantee of his suitability for the position he seeks. Many offers of help have been extended to him, and it is noteworthy that amongst his workers will be found Con- servatives and Liberals, Churchpeople and Non.- conformists. He is the only candidate whose business is in the ward.

The other two • candidates are exerting every effort, although in some quarters it is believed that one of them will withdraw on the date fixed for withdrawals next week.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
PENSIONER'S DEATH. Demise of Mrs. Mary Clare.

Through the death on Tuesday of Mrs. Mary Clare, of Sandy-lane, Lymm, that district lost one of its oldest inhabitants. The deceased, who was an old-age pensioner, was the wife of Mr. Thomas Clare, a local joiner, and had had a, long and honourable connection with the Broomedge Primitive Methodist Church. The funeral will take place to-day.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEVER SUNDAY SCHOLARS,

Broomedge Primitive Methodists.

The Primitive Methodists of Broomedge held their Sunday School anniversary on Sunday, when large congregations marked each service. The special preacher for the day was the Rev. W. paw, whose discourses were listened to with interest.

In the afternoon there was a special service, when special music was rendered by the scholars, who had been admirably trained by Mr. J. Perrin. The collections, which were in aid of the Sunday School, were equal to former years.

- Mr. E. Bonnell's Australian Experiences...... Next week we shall publish an interesting letter from Mr. E. Bonnell, grandson of the late Mr. John Bonnell, giving his experiences in New South Wales.

Cruelty to a Horse.—At the Warrington County Petty Sessions' on Wednesday, before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), and Messrs. C. E. Parker and J. Bailey, Robert Green, a carter, 31a, Winwick-street, Warring- ton, was charged with cruelty to a horse by working it in an unfit condition, and George Smith, 106, Evelyn-street, was charged with per- mitting it to be worked.—The evidence for the prosecution stated that the animal was very lame, suffering from a diseased knee on the off fore leg.—lt was stated by Smith that the horse was being given a rest, and if it did not im- prove it would not be worked again.—Smith was fined 20s. and costs, and Green was ordered to pay costs.

Will of Mrs. A. Coop.—The will of the late Mrs. Amelia Coop, of Brierfield, Penketh, has been proved at .11,250.

Mr. John Atherton.—Next week "The Exam- iner" will contain a special article dealing with the life and times of Mr. John Atherton, now between 80 and 90 years of age. Born in 1829, Mr. Atherton has witnessed many changes, and the article will he found to be of first-rate inter- est, not only locally, but generally.

Cycle Club.—The Cycling Club accepted the unusual weather conditions and journeyed to Knutsford on Saturday, where they hugely enjoyed themselves amongst the May Day revellers. The journey home was rather tiring, the wheelers being troubled by head and cross winds.
