===== 0002601_19140509_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News. -

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

The wedding ring and the dress coat are relics of barbarism. The former is a most noteworthy example of the survival of primitive customs. It was the ring by which the slave was dragged from the market after purchase by her flew owner. The dress coat, morning or eVening, is simply the garment worn by our ancestors when they went hunting. Because they were gentlemen they thought they had to ride in their long-tailed coats, so they split the coats up the back and cut them away in front. The buttons at the back were to hold up the sword belt.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
Incombustible Linoleum.

• To make an incombustible linoleum an experi- ment is being made by a manufacturer in Ger- many. He is adding magnesium carbonate to the pulp in the process of .manufacture. All the carbonates have the property of giving off carbon dioxide when heated at high tempera- tures. This, as is well known, is a very heavy gas, which does not rise, but creeps along the floor. For this reason in case of a lie it would tend to cut off the supply of air from linoleum. Magnesium carbonate is chosen because it breaks up at low temperatures.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
Feeding on Sunlight.

Major Miramond de la Roquette, of the French Army Medical Corps, in reporting to the Academie des Sciences the results of some obser- vations in Southern Algeria on the relation be- tween diet and sunlight, gave it as his opinion that the tissues of the human body directly utilise the radiant energy of the sunshine. The normal diet of the natives of hot and sunny climes is far lower both in quantity and in nutritive duality than that of inhabitants of less favoured regions, the sunlight seeming to make up the difference.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
Elie Rubies.

East Fife, which has re-elected our War Minister, probably possesses the largest toal- mining population in Scotland, next to remark. But Mr. Asquith is not concerned solely with "black diamonds,",for he can claim some con- nection with a more romantic industry. In chips athong the sea-gravel at Elie, and more rarely embedded in the trap-turfa, are found " pyropes," a variety of dark-red garnet. These pyropes have a little too much of the port wine shade of colour, but their lustre is very fine, and they are by far the most valuable of all Scot- tish gems, being sold under the name of "Elie rubies."

Tides Used for Land Reclaiming. The tides are very cleverly made to do the work of reclaiming land and building up farms aleng some of the rivers in the north-east of England. Describing this practice, of " warp- ing " to the Royal Agricultural Society, Mr. J. Stephenson states that allificial channels with sluice gates are provided, and twice a day in summer, at flood tide, the water carries inland a heavy load of "warp" or mud, to deposit on the low-lying swales. The land is made suitable for agriculture in two or three years, being' raised several feet when the flooding is discon- tinued.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
Letter Boxes on Street Cars.

Three cities of the, United States, Des Moines and Burlington, Indiana, and Grand Rapids, -ktichegan,'have been enjoying the benefits of street railway letter boxes for the past twelve years. Ordinary letter boxes are attached to the front or. rear of cars running on regular sche- Aides, and the mail is collected therefrom each time the cars pass 'the city post office.. The cars may be stopped by signal at any regular stop- ping place and mail deposited, whether the person so signalling intends to board the car or not.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
Camels v. Horses.

The camel is an alien in Europe; and the ex- periment which General Ratynski is trying of using them instead of horses for work on his farm in Samara will be watched with interest, says a writer in the "Pall Mall Gazette." The venture has been made before, but not in Rus- sia; and not, apparently, with much success. In 1829 the Marquis de Villafranca introduced to Spain, from the opposite African coast, a num- ber of camels with the idea of employing them in transport and agriculture. But local diffi- culties ensued—largely on account of the invin- cible antipathy of the hors towards their rivals—and in the end the newcomers were turned adrift in the marisma, where their descendants live wild to this day—one of the strangest adaptations of animal, life to unfami- liar environment. Who need despair when a camel can live in a marsh?

===== 0002601_19140509_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST —continued.

anglers will appreciate the quandary. Natur- ally, he wanted both those fish badly, but it was impossible for him to play them, so—brilliant idea—he decided to let them play themselves. Lie took a turn with both lines round the rypeck that moored the punt to keep the fish in clAck while he cut the lines from the rods and knotted them together. After •he had securely joined the lines together he released them from the rypeck, and chucked 'em overboard for a sort of tug-of-war. This the narrator is wont to detail with embellishments for which there is not room here, concluding thus—" Then the line parted in the middle. Did I lose 'em? Not much. You see, when the break came they were pulling across the narrowest part of the water, and the sudden recoil was so tremendous that the force of it shot both fish backward, clean out of the water, and a good six yards on to the grass. So I'd nothing to do but pick 'em up and carry 'em home."

- Just one more, and then I must cease for the present. Members of the Caledonian Society will be interested in the explanation that it comes from Scotland, though I am indebted for it to that inimitable and exhaustless raconteur and most enthusiastic and skilful of anglers, Mr. Carter Platts. "Yes," said the narrator, "it was undoubtedly the finest specimen ever taken out of the loch, and a most voracious creature, with a particular fancy for bric-a-brac and silver plate. The friend I was staying with Ilives on the very margin of the loch. One night a reservoir burst up on the hill, and flooded the loch so that the water stood four feet deep in my friend's dining-room, and when we came down to breakfast the following morning the pike was just helping himself to a silver tank- ard and a toast-rack off the sideboard. When he saw us he darted out of the window and got clear away. We tried a whole week to catch that fish, but he was too old for us, and he would have been in the loch yet if my friend's wife had not happened to drop her umbrella into the water. The pike opened his jaws and sucked it in point first. It was one of those patent spring umbrellas, that fly open automatically when you press the catch, and just when the pike had swallowed all but the handle his teeth touched the spring, and the thing expanded inside him. When he had got over his first shock of surprise he tried to disgorge the uncomfgrtable piece of mechanism, but the ribs had got entangled in his inner conscience, and he couldn't possibly spit the thing out. So we just hooked a boat- hook in the crooked handle of the umbrella and towed him ashore. When we opened him to get the umbrella out we found a whole British 'Museum and a sixpenny bazaar in his internal economy. I came away that day, but I heard afterwards that they had a jumble sale of the salvage, and raised £35 for the benefit of the poor of the parish."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
LAWS OF KENT WHICH DIFFER FROM THE LAWS OF ENGLAND.

AIiTHUR

The Men of Kent have been heard of in his- tory. According to an old story, William the Conqueror found it better to pacify than to fight them. ; And now by the latest turn in the whirligig of time they are out for a tussle with the House of Lords upon the very point for which they held out against the Norman invader.

According to the ancient tale, they threatened William of Normandy with fierce battle unless he guaranteed the continuance of their Kentish laws, which included the law of gavelkind, and if the story be true, William regarded com- promise as the better part of valour, and prom- ised not to inflict feudal law upon Kent. Until the present day Kent has had a "common law" which differs from that of the rest of England, and it is because this unique custom is threat- ened that Kentish men are banding themselves for wordy warfare. The subject has more interest for lawyers than for common folk, but several features have general interest First, there is the story; of which Northern readers have the privilege of believing as much as they choose.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
A STORY OF THE CONQUEST.

In explanation of the privileges enjoyed by the county of Kent, which are not shared by other counties, it is told that the Conqueror conceded them rather than risk a battle with the sturdy Kentish men: The story is told by Lambarde, who quotes as his authority Thomas Spot, a monk and chronicler of t. Augustine's, at Canterbury. It telrs that after the defeat of Harold and his Saxon warriors the common people of Kent took up weapons and met at Swanscombe, each man with a green bough, so that the Conatier2tr, on his approaching them, thought it was some *miraculous forest that moved towards him, but they, casting their boughs from them, sounded the trumpets, and showing their weapons, sent him a message offering him the choice of either peace or war— peace, if he -permitted them to enjoy their ancient liberties, or war, and that most deadly, if he denied them. Williarti wisely chose peace, and Kent kept its ancient Saxon laws. That the version is not to be dismissed as romance is evidenced in the fact that courts of law have taken it seriously and assumed that Kent has a common law of its own: Especially is this so in the case of the custom of gavel- kind.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT IS GAVELKIND?

In other English counties a man's freehold property, if, he dies intestate, passes to his eld- est son. In Kent it is divided equally among all his sons. This is pre-Conquest law, as dis- tinct. from that which was introduced by William of Nbrmandy, and its survival even in one corner of the land is of decided interest.

There are two explanations given of the mean- ing of the word " c.l,avelkind." The first derives it from "gafol," which means rent, and refers to rent and services rendered in respect of socage (that is, .free) lands. The other (which, we think, may be dismissed) derives it from "give all kind," that is, give to all male chil- dren alike.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME FEATURES OF GAVELKIND.

The chief feature of gavelkind is the one already mentioned. According to the general law of England, the eldest son takes all the landed' property of an intestate father, to the exclusion of the younger children. In Kent, the sons take equally between them. And if brothers, uncles or cousins were the nearest male heirs, they would take equally, and not the eldest alone.

Kentish law differs from English in the in- heritances of wife from husband and husband

===== 0002601_19140509_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHY.

This Month's Pilgrimage to

Lancaster.

BY SILVER-STREET.

Breadth is a pictorial quality as -desirable as its opposites—viz., patchiness, spottiness, dis- continuity—are undesirable, and, alas so fre- quent, in photography. "Avoid all subjects which glitter," said that keen critic, Harry Quilter, "because they are intensely difficult, and rarely satisfactory." The difficult part, if it stood alone, need not be final or fatal, but the fact is that the unsatisfactory part of the matter rests on sound physiological as well as aesthetic objections. When we walk quickly past a row of moderately close upright palings, behind• which is a strong light, the eye-strain effect is akin to that regarding many small glittering objects or a spotty picture. Such, for instance, as leaf-clad trees against a bright sky. —" A.P. and P.N." '

" CONTRAST° " PAPER. A new photographic printing paper with re- markable qualities has just been introduced. The paper, which is called " Contrasto,",is specially intended for printing from thin, flat, fogged, or over-exposed negatives, and there is not the least doubt that it will place great ad- ditional power in the hands of all photogra- phers for obtaining good prints from the class of negative named. Contrasto" is a collodio- chloride paper, and gives bright, strong prints from negatives that, in the ordinary way, would only yield flat, muddy results with any other printing process. It only needs a trial print to be made from a flat or fogged negative, and a comparison with an ordinary print from the same negative, to convince the user of the re- markable advantages the paper offers. -

By using this paper the need for intensifica- tion or otherwise strengthening thin or flat negatives is done away with, and the amateur photographer whose negatives do not produce prints of the requisite brightness is advised to obtain a supply of " Contrasto" forthwith. It is a printing-out paper, and is treated in a similar manner to ordinary P.0.P., but print- ing is carried very much further as the prints lose in the toning bath.

-- - - LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE PHOTO-

--- GRAPHIC UNION.

The annual excursion is fixed for Saturday, May 23. Lancaster is the favoured centre, on which the associates who join are to be con- gratulated, for hardly one of Lancashire's many towns can find so many subjects worthy of the camerist's notice. Three rambles are arranged for the day—one round the castle and town, one to Caton and the Lune Valley, and one to Williamson Park. For the 'convenience of those who use their cameras, dark rooms, are provided free at the Lancaster Photo Society's headquarters, and at the President's shop, whilst the comfort and refreshment of all will be catered for at the King's Arms Hotel. Altogether it should be a very successful gathering.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
SNAPSHOTS.

Our congratulations again to Mr. Harry Boseow, whose name appears under "Hon men- tion" in last week's "A.p. and P.N." Queen .Alexandra has long been known as an enthusiastic amateur photographer; but it is not so well known that Queen Mary is also a

devotee of the camera. She was seen on Easter Monday with a hand camera on the terrace of Windsor Castle, whence she made some snap- shots of the crowds of holiday-makers. The sensitive side of all photographic papers which have had a gelatine or collodion coat- ing—this includes p.0.p., gaslight, bromide, self- toning, collodio-chloride, &c.—can be recognised at once by the way the paper curls. However flat it may be on the whole, there is sure to be some little curl to it, and unless one has been at much trouble to breathe on it sufficiently to make it curl the reverse way (a thing that is not in the least likely to happen by accident), the curl is always with the coated or sensitive side inwards; that is to say, the face is concave, the back convex.—"Photog. and Focus."

OUNFIELD.

from wife. In common English law a \wife would have a life interest in one-third of any lands not disposed of b a husband's wilt; in Kent she has one-half so long as she remains Y chaste and unmarried. A. husband, under English common law, has a life interest in all the' lands of a deceased wife, provided there are children of the marriage capable of inheriting. In Kent a husband has a life interest in one-half of such lands, whether there be childrem or not, but only so long as he remains unmarried.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
ANCIENT KE.NTISH PRIVILEGES.

Some privileges that Kent has always enjoyed boywn a ownership Grand Assize gavelkind lands we in matters of land may also be briefly recorded. ansotto Assize of twelve knights, but by a jury of twelve men who were Disputes as holders of gavelkind land. Where a writ was issued against the holder of gavelkind land by his lord a peculiar form of words was used. This ancient form was written in a Kentish dialect, of which only a very capable anti- quarian could supply the meaning. • In ancient times the, general law was that a man could not dispose of his freehold lands by wilt. This was altered by an Act passed in the reign of Henry VIII.; but in Kent owners could always do so. In the rest of England, where a person was found guilty of felony the lands were forfeited to the Crown, but in Kent this was not so except , in, cases of treason. Of course, felons were I punished, but their lands went to their sons. The Kentish maxim was: " The father to the bough, the son to the plough." Another Kentish privilege has been that which allowed an infant owner (male or female) when of the age of 15 years to sell his or her land by feoffment (the ancient mode of conveying by means of symbolical delivery). Of course, these lirivi!eges do not count for much to-day, because .legislation has extended I similar or better privileges. • The point to notice is that Kent has enjoyed them all along, and that theY:have not been regarded as usages contrary to those of tile country, but true com- mon law of the land, applicable only to Kent. Attempts have been made to put Kent upon a level with the rest of the, nation. In 1828 a Royal Commission upon Real Property recom- mended the abolition of gavelkiqd, and in 1836 a Bill was introduced for that purpose. But Kent' was successfully indignant, and it was dropped.

KENTISH MEN RALLY FOR CONFLICT. ' Once again Kentish custom is threatened. Last year a Real Property Bill was introduced into the House of Lords by the Lord Chancel- lor, which provides for the total abolition of gavelkind. The •men of Kent and Kentish men are in arms, and a society has been formed to give them battle array. The Lord Chancellor has taken a large task if he proposes to argue them out of their privileges. As early as A.D. 1293, in the days of "King Edward the some of King Henrie,'l it stands on- record in the law-court French of the period: ",Ces sont. les vsages de Gauylekind e de Gauyle kendeys' en Kent, que furent , deuant Je Conquest, e en le-' Conquest, e totes honres ieskes en ca." (These are the usages of gavelkind and of gavelkind -Men in Kent, which were before the- Conquest; and at the ConqueSf,'Yhd ever since till now). Parliament did not originate Kentish law; it existed before there was any Parliament. And Kentish folk want to know why such a meddling mushroom institution as the British Parliament should be allowed to interfere with things that are older than itself: _

--- , It is a matter in which lawyers may differ, but antiquarians will be with them to a man.

Literary Gleanings.

JOYS OF THE SMALL COLLECTOR.

In "Great Thoughts" for May—the first num- ber of a new volume—there is an interesting interview with Sir James Yoxall, M.P., who, speaking of "the joys of the small collector," says: _ _

" Personally, I have never concentrated at all. I 'diffused,' if I may say so, and in that way I go to know .!-L good deal about a great many things—china, glass, medallions, Wedgwood, lustre-ware, furniture, brass, pictures, prints in black and white and colour and so on. A wise _rule for the beginner whose income is small is to fix a sum beyond which he will not go for any one thing.

"The first water colour drawing I ever bought cost me eighteenpence with two others. You can judge what I think of it when I tell you it is still in my drawing-room. Near it hangs a Turner water colour drawing which I picked up for sixpence. I found it in London; nay, more, in the very heart of London, in a portfolio in a neighbourhood where second-hand dealers throng, near Great Portland-street. MIGRATING BIRDS.

_ Mrs. Stuart Menzies writes in an intimate way about migrating birds in the "Lady's Pic- torial." "Some birds," the writer states, "are lazy fellows, and only move from one part of England to another. Redpolls, finches and robins come in quantities from Scotland, York- shire, Cumberland and other parts of the North to settle in Devonshire and Cornwall for the winter, where they hope to find plenty of sloes, oaun in ntaiiiluamshmebre.rries, hips and haws, &c. This is why in the winter we see so many more yel- low-hammers, robins, thrushes, and chaffinches

"It is difficult to imagine the number of birds , moving house in the, six weeks .they usually migrate, but we may form some sort of idea' from 15,000 ,skylarks having been caught in. one night at-the Heligoland Lighthouse, which is a great point for observation, so many birds pass along the coasts of the German Ocean; u them or the air tells them they seem to know that where adiglit-is.,:idmee, they will find a resting-place and food, so when a fog descends upon a ctorm is impending, they make at once for a lighthouse, which, though it helps many, is also the end of all things here for some, as they dash themselves, or get hurled by the wind, against the lights, and their own little light is put out for all time. The Heligoland Lighthouse is mostly visited by birds passing from Scandi- navia and Arctic Europe; Heligoland is about the same latitude as Scarborough. "Birds go to the Arctic regions to breed, not by thousands, but millions, because of the amount of food provided by Nature. The seed- eating fraternity find plentiful supplies of cran- berries and other such fruits which have been frozen all the winter, but the moment the snow melts, these food supplies are ready for them. "Generally speaking, September 15th is the date I look for my swallows and martins to col- lect on the church tower; they are followed in their flight by the nightingales and little fly- catchers; one of these latter flew into my room after food not long ago, and as I held the fragile, tender, frightened little thing in my hand to help it out again, I admired the little bright eye and exceedingly tender, delicate beak, and marvelled how the little construction of feathers and big heart could undertake those days and nights of ceaseless flying. Fly-catchers or their direct descendants return for years following to the same spot to nest. It may be the old birds have found it "Hallowed Ground," or it may be the children who have been brought up there and return to the Place where they found protection and love, where "everything was right because mother did it." The chiff-chaffs leave in October, also many wagtails, and robins, though, of course, not nearly all. " The wings of migratory birds are always pointed, another of Nature's wondrous works, thereby assisting them in the fulfilment of their inherited desires to migrate. Birds moult before they start away, and always seem ashamed of themselves, retiring songless amongst the trees, bushes and sheds. Some travel in groups, some in crowds and flocks; others, like the cuckoo and woodcock, are recluses and unsociable, prefer- ring to be quite alone, while those that pair for life always go together. "Birds accustomed .to migrate grow restless and miserable if kept in cages when the time ar- rives that they would in the ordinary course of events start away for other lands. The cuckoo will stand no nonsense, but promptly droops and dies. This bird is worth some study; the first to leave our shores, with its lovely vice, and its unlovely character, we all look forward to hearing it in the spring. "One hundred and eighty different birds breed in our country and migrate. While many of our summer friends are 'leaving us, others take their place. Soon we shall have flocks of field- fares flying over from Norway and Sweden; they are easily recognised by the way they wheel about in parties of 40 and 50 in the air. Then battalions of starlings come from Norway and Germany in the winter; they are cheery, sociable little fellows in their kaleidoscopic coats; whatever light you see them in, the colour is different—purple, green, blue, all blended. They like to attach themselves to the army of rooks, and join heartily in the general conversation, having none of the shyness of their fellow travellers, the field-fares, who prefer their own society."

ACTION AGAINST GUN-RUNNERS.

YOUNG LIBERALS AND THE ULSTER QUESTION.

At the quarterly couuril meeting of the Lan- cashire, Cheshire and District Federation of the League of Young Liberals, held at the Re- form Club, Manchester, on Saturday, the recent developments in the Home Rule question were discussed, and a series of propositions relating to the Army crisis Isere agreed to. Councillor M. E. Mitchell presided and re- iterated the view expressed by him in his presi- dential address that he delivered three months ago, that the Government had taken the wisest course in waiting to see what the Ulstermen would do and the worst they could do. It had brought evils in its train, but he was firmly convinced it would also bring great good. The gun-running and the -attempt to destroy the loyalty of the Army for party purposes showed the length. to which they would go. The - good that haa been done by this latter incident waslo make the country seeand realise .what a terrible thing it was to have the Army governed by the classes. The ability to swagger should not be the qualification for a commission, butl the ability fe givelliod and efficient service. (Hear, hear.) The two days' debate in the House on Mr. Bonar Laws' motion had shown that the leader of the Opposition was more dis- credited than ever both in the House and within his own party. (Applause.) A lengthy discussion took place on the subject of Government action against the gun-runners, one section calling for drastic steps to vindicate the law.

_ Mr. Lister, of Birkenhead, submitted a reso- lution to this effect, but Mr. G. G. Armstrong pointed out that while they might call for the rich to be dealt with by the law the same as the poor they were not at all sure that if pro- ceedings were now taken it would be the rich and not the poor that would suffer They were dealing with a body of men who were the dupes of unscrupulous leaders, and-it would be these people rather than the wealthy leaders that would probably suffer. The gun-running had come as such a shock to the Unionists as to cause vigorous private remonstrances, and it was not likely to be repeated. That being so. were they sure they should be angry with the Government for not going bald-headed for the gun-runners? Mr. W. Edge, prospective Liberal candidate for Ashton-under-Lyne, warmly supported this - view, and ultimately the following resolution was carried:

"That this conference views with great alarm the illegal actions which have taken place in Ulster, and assures the Government that any action they may take will receive our enthusi- astic support."

HOW COULD SHE?

A gentleman met a child crying in the street, and said : " Now be a good boy and stop crying." "I can't," sobbed the child. "Here's a penny for you," said the gentle- man. "Why can't you stop crying and be a good boy?" "Cause I'm a girl," was the answer.

CINDERELLA TRACED TO EGYPT. The old story of Cinderella was, of course, not invented by the Brothers Grimm, says the "Hamburger Naehrichten." William Grimm took it down from the lips of the old "story woman," to whom he was indebted for so many treasures, and he worked into it several other additions of his own discovery. The origin of Cinderella, which crops up in the Pentameron of Basil, the graceful pages of Per- rault, as well as Norwegian, Hungarian, and Servian literature, can be traced to the ancient legends of Greece, as Messrs. Hansrath and Marx proved in a volume published by Eugene Diederichs at Jena. The Cinderella of ancient Greece was discovered by the enamoured King through the instrumentality of her shoe, just as she is in the modern version. But she was no shy and tender maiden, but a beautiful woman, whose story the Roman orator Claudius Aelianus, writing in Greek in the year 200 A.D., traced to a very old Egyptian-Greek narrative. Even Herodotus, in his early days, knew of a personality not unlike that of the story. Ac- cording to Aelianus's account, the beautiful Rhodopis, whose fame extended through all Egypt, was bathing one day in the Nile, and her servants were taking care of her clothes. "Then an eagle swooped down, seized one of her shoes and flew with his capture back so Memphis, where King Psammetiehus held his Court, and let the shoe fall into the King's lap. The grace- ful lines of the shoe, and the delicate work en it, excited the King's curiosity no less than did amongst the the bird's act, and he gave command that sear' li should be made through all the country for the wearer of the shoe. She was fount and 102. iaaorni ad b jlioe hearth custom, ars rt sot e mv , eyi n e a n rost, enter ancient de i e made her his wife." Tho narrative goes on to say that Rhodopis, d after living and ruling by her husband's side int t great i th as happinessliinvariable vs f tie y h doesn y and was buried in the third great pyramid of Qjiizeh The idea of a dirty maiden living at all into the story of this beautiful woman' stories, for woe-begone people to sit amongst ashes, and to throw ashes over your clothes was a sign of misery and humility. Odysseus, for instance, after he had taken refuge as a dis- tressed wanderer in the house of Alcinous, at himself down right humbly in ihe ashes of the hearth.

ST. HELENS CHARITIES. HEAVY COST OF ADMINISTRATION.

The question as to whether the St. Helens Charity Organisation Society should be con- tinued or wound up was discussed on Monday at the yearly meeting, presided over by the Mayor (Sir David Gamble). - Mr. F. Dromgoole, hon. secretary, read the report of the Executive Committee, which stated that there was no lack of employment in St. Helens during the past year for any able-bodied persons willing to work. That state of things had caused the demand upon the funds of the society to be exceptionally small, and the amount granted in relief by the society during the past year was the smallest on record. The number of applications dealt with were 69, of which 51 were relieved, 13 were declared "no cases," and 5 were sent to the Guardians. Forty-nine private inquiries had been made for the purpose of private charity. Mr. David Forster (hon. treasurer) reported that .t 8 us. 6d. had been received in subscrip- tions and .t5O from a legacy by the late Dr. Gaskell. Relief to the extent of ...tll us. 7d. and clothing valued about .R 3 had been given; the debit balance of £36 19s. had been wiped off, and ~Q 76 19s. spent on administration, leaving a balance in hand of AG Is. 9d. FEW APPLICATIONS.

The Mayor said there was always a difficulty about comparing the amount of relief and the cost of administration, but they had compara- tively few applications during the year. The need for investigation was 'brought home to them time after time. The subscription list was going dawn, and .peeple were under the impres- sion that the Insurance Act was supplying the need. Certain firms in the town who hAd been subscribers said they would have nothing more to do with the society. M. Dromgoole suggested that steps be taken to enlarge the scope of the" society, and if possibly arrangements should be made to Yr.:irk with the ladies' charity, the N.S.P.C.C., &c•, and that a Ladies' Committee be formed. Chief _Constable Ellerington said that all the information gained in connection with the police-aided clothing scheme was always at the disposal of the society's agent, and if they knew that he had- previously dealt with a case they conferred with him. Miss Mills, from the Central Society, London, said it was found that the society was still needed to help the families who were onlY re- ceiving 10s. from the National Insurance when the father was ill. It was decided to form a committee to con- sider the position as suggested.

WORK-A-DAY WONDERS.

IV.-TELLS OF A FAMOUS EXPERIMENT IN FEATHER-DYEING,

"I'm not a CONFIDENT woman," said Patty, " and never tihall be: So when it came to keep- ing my promise about helpinc, Mtg.,' Hendry- Diamond-dye her feathers-4.11X sashlibboiilsl fair trembled within myself. , So I asked her to let me manage the dyeing part alone,• thinking I could get on better just by myself. - "'I don't know why you're so nervous,' said she, for I've been reading right through the Diamond Dye Direction Book the chemist gave me when I bought these two threepenny packets of silk-and-wool dye, and everything seems so simple. Please don't be nervous.' " Well—l took the feathers and ribbons in home, and laid them out on the parlour table, and who do you think should come in but Mr. Garrett, the minister? And I had to tell, him all the story—he was that curious. I brought down my skirt and showed him, and told him about Mrs. Finch's silk blouse and Mrs. Bridges' linen, and I don't believe he ever en- joyed a talk better. 'All my fears went out of the door along with him, and I just set to work with the feathers and the Diamond Dyes, and in about an hour I ran along to tell Mrs. Hendry that the feathers , were done all but curling. They were two ' beauties—and I tell you they DID look nice and the ribbon matched them exactly. We were; making merry over our success when back came the minister, and, 'Mrs. Prue,' said he, 'Mrs. Garrett wants to know if you will come up to the Manse on Friday, and have a Diamond-dye- ing day with her.' BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN SUNDAY- SCHOOL. The anniversary of the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Sunday School was held on Sunday, when the Rev. J. Grange Bennett occupied the pulpit. In the afternoon a musical service was rendered by the children and choir. Mr. John Jackson, of Moore, acted as chairman, and an address was given by Mrs. James Dodds. In the evening an organ recital was given by Mr. W. T. Pearce, with Mr. W. Hipkiss as conductor. 41 "GOD OF RITUAL."

Mr. C. P. Pitt, of the Lancashire College, occupied the pulpit at Wycliffe Church on Sun- day morning In the course of his sermon, which he based upon Joshua v. 15, "Loose thy shoe from off thy foot; for the place whereon thou standest is holy," the preacher remarked that there was nowadays a tendency to make a god of the ritual of our worship, not its sim- plicity. Ile concluded that we must consecrate the fact of the present to the fact of God: to- morrow was not a fact. In the evening Mr. Pitt conducted the service at Elmwood-avenue Chapel.

AN ARTISTIC LOAL PREACHER.

Warrington possesses a rather notel statue modelled in cement, the work of Mr. M. Osborne, of 'Snntsford-road. The statue represents "The ' Christian Soldier," fully equipped with the, helmet of salvation, the shield of faith, and tlie girdle of truth. He is shod with the prepara- tion of the gospel of peace, and ready to „fight with the sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God. The idea of the modelist was that the striking work should he' the means of doing good, reminding those who saw it of the neces- sity of arming themselves against the enemy, sin. Mr. Osborne is in his 70th year, is a local preacher on thd Wesleyan Methodist plan, and has erected the statue in his garden,

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The Rev. T. T. James delivered his monthly address to the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sun- day, when, Mr. H. Osborne presided. Taking for his subject "My thoughts," the speaker said that the very changeable weather they had had rendered the preparation of an address on that season of the year a difficult task. He once prepared one on the beautiful spring weather, dud when he delivered it, the weather might have been winter. (Laughter.) However, the winter was now past, and this was the time to get out into the country, and see God's glory in the new life appearing. The sight would inspire them to bring a little of that glory into the town.

The lesson was read by Mr. McEachern, and Mr. B. Partar, the secretary, led.• the prayer. Miss E. Hewitt sang " 0 Divine Redeemer" very sweetly, Mr: Payton accompanying on the organ.

By MARJORIE. -

Women as Lawyers. There is a possibility that the Bill now before Parliament to admit women to the legal pro- fession and to permit them to practise as solici- tors, will pass the House of Commons and be- come law. Lord Haldane, Mr. Asquith and the Law Officers agree with the principle of the Bill, and although-not pledged to support it, the Gov- ernment will show. itself friendly to it. As a proof—if one were needed—of the instructive conservatism of the legal profession, the Court of Appeal in December last refused to reverse the practice of five centuries, although reminded that in past days a woman had acted as Con- stable of England and Great Chamberlain, and that she might be a churchwarden and a sexton.

A Woman Sea Captain. Denmark has always been a woman's para- dise, and there is no profession or calling Dan- ish women may not follow on equal terms with men. But it is only in recent times that a woman—even in Denmark—has sailed the seas as commander of an ocean passenger liner. This distinction is enjoyed by Frau Bandenz, whose husband accompanies her in the subordinate position of a mere ship's doctor. Frau Bandenz has from childhood been keenly interested in steam engines and navigation. So thoroughly had she mastered her subject that the examina- tion for a captain's certificate was a mere baga- telle. It was at first doubtful if insurances could be effec.ted on a ship captained by a woman, but that matter being satisfactorily arranged, the lady's certificate as captain, was endorsed, and a ship found for her. She has had experience in home waters as cgmmander, but this will be her first voyage in that capacity to America. A flemarkable Record. The courage and endurance of modern sports- women is in every respect equal to that of the hardiest and most experienced of masculine hunters of even big game. Perhaps the record of Mrs. Holmes Tarn, who has just returned

'HEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Care of the Baby.

'First and foremost for the unfolding of brawn and brain, the child must have food, the food must contain all the materials needed by all the different structures of the body, and these materials must be in approximately the pro- portions needed, says Dr. James Rogers in "Good Health." It is astonishing how the body, even an immature body, will adjust itself to ill-pro- portioned foods; but though it can transmute compounds.to a certain extent, it i not prepared to do miracles—it cannot create chemical ele- ments nor make good ill-balanced proportions of elements. Babies are by no means alike, butdiffer greatly as to their degree of perfectness of organs and balancing of functions; and while most of them arrive in conditiob for thriving 'on the nutri- ment which nature intended they should re- ceive,' mine of them' are prepared ter selecti.their : eller friirct :among the foodstaffs in. the (-world about thorn. Vnfortunately, mothqrs also are not usually ready to make a wise;sele,ction for their offspring; and as no other food is quite equal, for baby purposes, to mother's milk, it follows that most, and especially the delicate, are likely to show (by digestive disturbances or slow growth) some disappointment under the process of artificial feeding. The Child's Play. - .

A child should never be urged td walk or to exercise in other ways. Bodily movements come spontaneously with the development of the organs concerned. The child should never be unduly excited to play. Play is the result, the outward manifestation, of superfluous nervous and muscular energy. If it does not play of its own accord, it is an indication of the need of rest or of better nourishment.

_ _ Rest is as-important as exercise, or even more important, because adequate rest must precede exercise. Exercise has been carried too far if the child does not sleep profoundly afterward. Children are especially faulty nowadays in hav- ing ill-balanced or damaged nervous systems, and nothing else can prevent or, help this con- dition, aside from plenty of good food, as much as abundant rest. If there is a quiet place about the house, that should be the baby's sleeping quarters, and he should not only have quiet, but freedom from strong light.

To her great disappointment she failed to kill one, but she has shot rhinoceros, and spent long hours and wearisome nights watch- ing for panthers,' and has travelled more miles on the back of an elephant than she cares to count. The Clumsy Cape.

- - - - If the -millinery of the hour is More, suited to the youthful than the elderly, mUch the same may be said of the ugly bustle draperies, which very ,few women over twenty-five can wear suc- dency. in the direction of clumsiness, and on any cessfully. The capes, too, show a marked ten- but the tallest and slimest figures are by no means graceful. However this may be., that they have " caught on" is indisputable, and as fresh designs appear every week, the needs of the short and full figure will doubtless receive attention from those whose duty it is to provide for every type of wearer. But the cape and the full tunic have wrought a marked change in silhouette, and introduced freer lines in every direction. The pegtoi effect at the foot of skirts will probably last through the shimmer, but its day is almost over, and it will be seen no more among smart ktyles next autumn.

The New Sunshades. This year's sunshades, in common with modes and styles, exhibit an unusual variety, so that the choice of something becoming and suitable is within the reach of even the most exacting. The en tout cas and umbrella' are as attenuated a§ it is pogible to make them, but not so the parasol, which is festooned with filmy net and chiffon, and bedecked with ribbon frills and tucks, and all kinds of delicate and fanciful decorations. These are, however, not intended for ordinary wear, for which they are too ex- pensive and too -ornate. Printed cotton sun- shades that have been prepared for everyday use, are delightful in design (generally floral), and are very moderate in price. Flat Japanese shapes are also likely to find fayour. Not much eccentricity is hown in handles, many of which are made from a novel imitation of tortoiseshell, used with wood. Birds' heads, more especially owls and ducks, and some animals, the cat in particular, are among the most fanciful.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
The Waistcoat and Other Blouses.

- - - A very picturesque garment is the waistcoat blouse, made in striped silk, or materials of a transparent nature, such as gauze or crepe de chine. It is fastened at the waist by two large buttons, and being cut away, and longer in front than at the back, forms two points under the buttons. This type is useful for wearing over blouses that are no longer quite fresh. In shape it is loose, and quite baggy under the arms. Some women find an all-white blouse very try- ing; when this is the case, a little roll collar and vest or tiny waistcoat of Pompadour silk, will be discovered to effect quite a transforma- tion, and to give a becoming note of colour. Many lingerie blouses have collars of Futurist designs in silk, but these are not as effective as the pretty floral patterns. The knell of the blouse has been sounded many a time and oft, but still it takes on fresh life, and increases in popularity. Those women who are clever in copying models could make at least six for the price of two bought in a shop. An easily made and very effective blouse was oi ivory-tinted shadow .lace mounted on ninon, with frills of lace at neck and sleeves, and a high collar and belt of striped brocade. Flowers and Fruit.

Although there is much variety in the mil- linery of the moment, all the-new hats resemble each other in their lavish display of flowers as trimming, and in the brilliance of their colour- ing. ===== 0002601_19140509_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
COOKERY CORNER.

Some Attractive Recipes.

To make apple and currant roly-poly use 6oz. of currants, six largo cooking apples, ill). brown or castor sugar, the rind of a lemon chopped finely, a little nutmeg. Method: Pare, core, and cut the apples into slices, put them into a saucepan with the lemon rind and sugar, cook till soft,, and add the nutmeg. Have ready a rather rich suet crust, roll it out thinly, spread the apples over the paste, sprinkle over the currants, roll the pudding up, ,closing the ends properly, tie in a floured cloth, and boil for two hours.

Dainty Spring Soup. This is not only nutritious, but is very light, and takes, but a few minutes to make. Shako 2 ounces of fine sago into 1 quart of boiling water, and stir instantly and quickly, to pre- vent lumps. Add a small onion, and boil for 10 4Linutes:,-Take,out, the, onion,: stir in 3 teaspoon- fuls -of remto, Meat extract ; 4ake tho' pan from the fire, and stir in 2 raw yolks of eggs *ell beaten; stir rapidly to mix well, place the pan over the fire again to heat up, but the contents must not boil. Season with salt, and pepper, and serve at once. Cornflour Cake. _ _

- - ---.--. Take 3oz. butter, Vb. powdered sugar, 3 eggs, 1 tablespoonful flour, /lb. cornflour, / teaspoon- ful each tartaric acid and carbonate of soda (dissolved in milk), a few carraway seeds. Beat the butter to a cream, and add the sugar and eggs. Beat all again; mix in the flour the seeds, and the tartaric acid and dissolved soda. Mix well, and bake in a buttered tin, for about three- quarters of an hour. The seeds may be left out if not cared for.

Tomato Ketchup. Cut up three and a half pounds of tomatoes, and one pound and a half of sour apples (weighed after peeling and coring) and two small onions, add two ounces of ground ginger, an ounce and a half of mustard seed, and half an ounce of whole pepper (these last three tied together in muslin loosely and boiled in the fruit for half an hour to three-quarters and then removed). Add half a pound of brown sugar, one pint of vinegar, and cook all to- gether until the fruit is tender. Stir occasion- ally to keep it from burning. Pass all through a sieve, taking the muslin bag of mustard' seed, &c., out when the fruit has cooked 45 minutes as already stated. Put into bottles or jars and cover securely like jam. This will keep any length of time in a cool place. Volk

Time lable for Nursing.

A rough outline of the day's routine on a monthly nurse would be something like this: 6 a.m.—Take baby in for his feed. Make early' cup of tea for yourself and patient, go to your own bath. Some nurses like to bath baby be- fore breakfast, too. 7.4s.—Wash the patient's face and hands. B.—Babi's feed. B.ls.—Break- fast. B.4s.—Let th 6 husband see the patient for 15 minutes. 9.---. Patient's toilet, blanket bath, lotion -wash,. 44c. 10.—Baby's feed. Let the maid come- in and-do the room up, or do, it yourself if it is your -duty. Make up chart ready for doctor; 11.—Bath--baby and wash the nap. king if ,necessarY. l'4l2.—Baby's -feed. . Take baby out. (If the baby is on bottles you must prepare_ the feeds at this time.) I.—Dinner. I.3o.—Lotion wash. '2.—Baby's feed. Let ,the patient sleep until -fatir o'clock, and, take a rest yourself. 4.—Baby's feed. Wash patient's ,face and -ha.nds;'lttioar, 4.3o.—Tea. 5.30.-4.0t10n wash. 6.—Baby's-feed: Top and tail him, and settle. him for .the-night. 7.—Patient's :supper. B.—Baby's feed; .prare patient for the night. 9.—Get ready for 'bed and- take baby in. for his feed at 10 p.m. °

Method is rpeessaryj without it there will be no comfort either for you or your patient, but be- ware, on the other hand, of making a fetish of routine. Do not ,imagine the sky will fall if once in awhile you 'are unable to bath the baby early and have to do it after the doctor's visit. Or if the patient wants to have a chat with her husband just when% you had arranged to make her bed, don't make a trouble of it, but give way cheerfully. It is much more important that the patient should be happy than that the room should be done to the minute.—" Nursing Times."

===== 0002601_19140509_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
Deafness.

Deafness at all times of life is not easy to cope with. The mechanism of the internal ear is so delicate that it is easily put out of gear and most difficult to rectify. Inflammation or simply uric acid or other sandy deposit, like rust, stiffens the extremely minute joints so that sound vibrations are not so easily conveyed to the registering apparatus in the brain, and hence are blurred or lost altogether unless very loud, as a door stiff on its hinges requires a much greater effort to open than when they are well lubricated. The best that can be hoped for is that by an alteration of the habits recent deposits may be removed, but in old age even this cannot be hoped for. NNW

NOIIII/4 NOW WIN VOilk VOA Walk VOW VI" Wolk "A STRIKING CIIARACTER."

The Rev. 11. Ogden's Pointed Sermon. The Rev. 11. Ogden, of Golborne-street Bap- tist Church, preached on Sunday evening from the subject "A Striking Character," taking as his text Genesis xii. 2: "And I will make of thee a great nation, and I will bless thee, and make thy name great; and thpu shalt be a blessing." The preacher went on to say that Nature had a quick way with the weak and the unfit. One scientist declared that Nature is read in tooth and in claw. One has but to watch by the edge of some pool to see that statement about Nature justified. Any animal that cannot adjust itself to new environments must pay the penalty for its want of adaptability. That law is equally imperative in the spiritual realm, both in nations and in individuals. God has pulled down the mighty from their seat, and exalted 'them of low degree. What prophet could have foretold in the days when the Roman power flouxished that the dwellers in the tin isles would build' upon the ruins of the mighty Roman Empire? There is a law in the life of nations and individuals, as well us in Nature, and that law is the survival of the fittest.

, It was said of Abraham that God would make of him a great nation, and we ought to be able to trace in his life the operation of those laws that prove the survival of the fittest. A very striking characteristic of Abrandm was his simple obedience and child-like piety in relation to God. Blatchford has jeered aiothe narrative which outlines for us the story of the offering up of Isaac. But what is most important in that story is the obedience of Abraham. At the outsat of this chapter ho was commanded by God to get out from his country and his kindred. He went at a period of life when most men are considering an honourable retirement. Few men of 75 ,commence new schemes, or found new dynasties or create new nations. Abraham might have pleaded the right to an old-age pension, and rest and comfort. With child-like obedience he obeyed the command of God, and became the founder of a new nation.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL.

Mrs. Dodds and Boys who get "Too Big."

Every available seat was occupied in the Parr Hall on Saturday evening, when the Band of Hope May Festival was repeated. Mrs. Dodds presided,' and at the outset was supported by the Rev. E. Morgan, Mr. T. Dale, and Mr. Gandy.

Mrs. DODDS expt4ssed her Pleasure at once more presiding over a Band of Hope, Festival. After etulogising ..on the patience, sacrifice and love of those who had trained the. children so perfectly, she said the children were living examples of all that had been done in their training. The people who had worked hard all through the year were the ones who should re- ceive the praise for their efforts. The Mayor had made reference at the previous gathering to the small number of boys in the Band of Hope. It was one of the saddest things in the Band of Irope that boys became too big in their way to continue Band of Hope boys. She might tell them that they would never grow out of it, and by sticking to it they would be helped. in a way they had no power of realising. She asked all the Band of Hope boys to help as workers, and so take on the work where the older ones left off.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
Please the World at Large.

If • they did that they would not only please themselves, but the world at large. (Hear, hear.) They would also help to build up the future, because they, would be the men and women of the future. She would ask them to make up their minds not to consider themselves too big to help in the work, for they had more influence than they thought. They should use it in help- ing to spread temperance in the town and put- ting down one of the greatest curses the world had ever known. (Applause.)

Mr. GANDY said the receipts were higher than last year, but also the .expenses were more, so that they had only made a small profit. When the crowning ceremony hadTheen per- formed, Mrs. DODDS presented the "Queen" with a gold brooch, and Miss HILDA TAYLOR, on behalf of the Working Men's Mission Band of Hope, presented a book.

The organist, Mr. T. Tanner, and Mr. Spruce, the conductor, were responsible for a large amount of splendid work, and are deserving of great thanks.

Education and Refinement.

One of the greatest blessings of modern, civil- isation is the spread of education and refine- ment. Coarseness in feeding no longer qppeals to the multitude. It is singujarly fitting that Greece, the land of classicism, culture, poetry; and art, should have contributed largely to the refining influence of the world's dietary. Greece, it is, that provides the Currant, tho delicious fruit of its sun-kissed vine—the fruit that is associated with all the glorious memories of the land of Greece, its philosephers, its poets, and its 'artists. The delicionS little .Currant is in- deed worthy of: its beautiful country, for it is full of grape sugar, the purest of ,nutriment; It is savoliry iii the extreme, and thoroughly 'nourishes the body without producing the Coarseness and irritability brought on ,by an excess of flesh-diet. Use the Currant daily. Let it be a, regular feature of the homely bill of fare, for the dainty fruit is conducive' to health and refinement. Here is one way of enjoying it.

CURRANT SODA CAKE.

lb. Currants, 1 lb. flour, lb. brown or moist sugar, lb. butter, 2 eggs, 2 teaspoonfuls treacle, 1 teaspoonful bi-carbonate of soda, pint milk. Method: Mix the flour and soda, rub in the butter, add sugar and Currants, then the treacle, milk, and beaten eggs. Beat all well together, fill in two well-buttered cake moulds, and bake in a moderately hot oven for about hours.

ROUMANIAN CONSOLIDATED OILFIELDS.

The "Financial News" (London) congratu- lates the Roumanian Consolidated Oilfields on their success in drilling through the Pontie formation and striking a " gusher " at 2,506 feet. It says :—" The well has justified the good judgment of the management by coming in at the rate of 500 tons a day, and, inasmuch as the oil horizons above the Pontic have been most prolific on the Bana-Moreni field, there is reason to believe that a long additional life has been given to the field. Other wells will now be taken down• to the new source, and they should prove highly remunerative."

There was no getting to Abraham's cheque book until you had CENsuRED THEmsELyEs, passed his prayer-book. A religion that cannot dominitte life is a faith that is paralysed. Of some American business men it is said that when they lock their office door they lock their business morals in their safe, and return home to open with their domestic key another and a different code of morals. We are indebted to a school of teachers who have insisted upon the solidarity of life.

The misery of the poor will always menace the peace of the rich. Whitechapel will always cast its shadow over Park Row, and as sure as businesses corrupt sooner or later the domestic life will fester and putrefy. Abraham added a practical life to his Piety. He also took the greatest care to preserve his religion. We sometimes delude ourselves into believing that churches and Sunday schools, ministers and Sunda school teachers, will preserve our reli- gion for us. And yet most of their labour is in "in, except the home be pious and God-like. Our streets are largely a replica of our domestic life. The streets .evidence the teaching of the home. Abraham would spend hours a week in teaching to his son Isaac the law, and the commandments of God. His servants would learn their religion from their master, and thus he would ensure' the perpetuity of his faith. Nature, as has been said, is read in tooth and claw. _ _

She is careless about the individual, very anxious about the type. In the realm of the Spirit there is less concern about any one Church than there is about preserving the spirit of God amongst men. Churches of any faith are only a service, in so far as they have a message unto men. And the Church of the day that will survive is the Church that meets the deep, urgent desires of the men and women of its day, and the deep, urgent desircs of the times.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CIIURCH

The Rev. H. Williamson occupied the pulpit on Sunday evening, and spoke on "Goodness which is beautiful," basing his remarks upon the text "Let your light so shine before men that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father which is in Heaven." The most important part of the text was the last clause, which went beyond the demands of the context. Some people practised their good works merely to seek glory from their fellow men. It was not enough to perform good works even though praise was not sought. Every action must have a touch of the Infinite; must suggest the Unseen; and tend to awaken the appeal of God in the heart of the beneficiary. The works of Christ spoke the language of love itself, and had a moral and spiritual grandeur about them. Ministrations among the needy were often done in a manner that robbed them of any charm. Our administration of the poor law had no spiritual influence on the recipients of the bounty. The charity was performed as a duty and not as an act of love. Individuals, who boasted of their outspoken- ness, and were proud of the directness of their speech, often reduced that quality to mere vul- garity. The listener was repelled fiequently by the truth, because its beauty had been de- stroyed and its glory had perished in the tell- ing. There would always be a falling short if duty or mere conscientiousness guided the action. • When duty was transfigured by love's radiance, then good 'works won a charm and an attractiveness which made them touch the heart of those who benefited.

STOCKTON HEATH WOMEN'S.

Mrs. Robert. Pemberton gave an address on the life of Florence Nightingale. The speaker pointed out how Miss Nightingale gave her life to the cause of nursing Soldiers in time of war. When she had no appliances for nursing, she, and her forty women helpers, commandeered Army stores for the purpose. Several of the members joined Sbi the discussion afterwards. The President, in closing, said that the influ- ence of Florence Nightingale's life gave an ex- ample for all Adult School membgrs—to live with the object of helping one another.

FLANNELETTE QUESTION.

Two St Helens Women Fined.

Two women shopkeepers at St. Helens were fined 20s. and costs each on Monday. on a charge under the Fabrics Misdescription Act of selling inflammable flannelette as non-inflammable.

In the case of Ann Forster, draper, 33, West- field-street, Mr. T. Garner, who defended, said the business was carried on by her son-in-law. Miss Woreley, an assistant in the Medical Officer's department, stated that on March 30 she visited the shop 'and bought a yard of flan- nelette. She asked the assistant in the shop if the stuff would not burn, and she said it would not. She further requested the assistant to write this on the receipt, which she did.

Mr. Garner iuggested that witness carefully framed the questions before going to the shop, with the view of trapping the assistant.

Witness: No.

Miss Denman, another assistant from the same department, having corroborated, Mr. Garner said the ratepayers of the, town protested most strongly against being trapped in this way. , The clerk: She has to carry out her duty. _ _ __

Mr. Garner: .Was it any part of her duty to trap these People? Miss Penman: I call it finding out whether they sell it or not. Continuing, witness ad- mitted she was told at Forgter's shop that the manager had instructed the assistants not tp sell any flannelette as non-inflammable. Dr. Cates, medical officer, gave evidences to the material bursting into flame when tested.

The Clerk remarked that to protect themselves the retail traders would have to force the manu- facturers to give them‘a written watranty that the good,s were up to the Home Office standard. They ought to be prosecuted in cases like these, as well as the retailers, ' . .

The manager of the shop and, the assistants gave evidence that instructions had been given for no flannelette to be sold as non-inflammable. Mary Thompson, of Higher Parr-street, was the other shopkeeper fined.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST SYNOD.

The twenty-fourth yearly Synod of the Liver- pool District Primitive Methodist Connexion was held at Ellesmere Port on Saturday. The name of the Rev. J. "Watkin (Birkenhead) for the presidency was submitted by the Rev. J. T. Barkby (Southport), and carried unanimously. Mr. D. Lewis (Widnes) was unanimously elected vice-president. The new President was pre- sented with a bouquet by Miss Winnie Barlow. Liverpool (third), Preston (second), Ramsey, Southport (first), and Clitheroe invited the Synod for next year. Southport secured the honour with 32 votes, • Liverpool coming second with 29.

The report of the Building Committee showed that a new school chapel had been erected at New Elerry at a .cost of ,t 1,410 a new church, purchased. at Blackpool costing £1,550, while classrooms had been, built at Marsliside, South-, port, /at a 'ccist of £280.' A site of land had been purchased at Buckley, Little Neston, and Kirk Michael (Isle- of Man), and various applica- tions for loans 'had been' sanctioned.

The report of the 'Missionary Committee showed that great interest had been aroused in the work of missions du'ring the year. The in- come was £1,054, an increase of £lB5.

MACARONI MOULD.

Boil some macaropi till tender in some slightly salted water, and, having strained off the liquor, mix it with a:dittle cream, a- small piece of butter, and 'seine grated cheese. Pound -and press through a sieve Some good firm tomatoes, half a large onion and a slice of raw ham or bacon; add to this a little gravy and mix these with a "farce" made from. slices of boiled Meat, herbs, and an egg or two, seasoned and moistened with a little cream. Fill a snipoth mould With alternate layers of macaroni and meat, moistening occasionally with a little tomato sauce; let the last layer be one of mac- aroni. Brush this over with some melted butter mixed with an egg; sprinkle with bread crumbs, and bake for thirty minutes. Turn it out and serve with a sauce.

GOOD SORT OF FELLOW. That the bay "gets along" with the food given him may not signify that it is just what he most needs. On the whole, the average baby is a good sort of a fellow, and doesn't complain much unless things are very bad. It would be better for him sometimes if he complained more. The bottle-fed baby is at- the mercy of his parents, whose love may not be Winced 133' their intelligence. When it comes to food, the best is not too good for the baby, nor is it ever too expensive; for the child demands only sub- stantials and wishes no luxuries in its diet.

e "TANNED" BY TANNERS.

The. Whitecrds' s Engineers did not relish the "tanning" they received to the tune of 165 to 112:from the Warrington Tannery team. There were only three of the Engineers who won their individual games, and these :were really good triumphs, but three or four men cannot-play a whole „team—at least not a team like the one from the Tannery, which can boast some good men.. However, it must be taken into account that the victors gained 20 points by two walk- overs.

COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

Success of Warrington Teams.

Everyone must be glad that once again War- rington teams are making their presence felt in the Club Championship Competition organ- ised by the Lancashire Association. On Satur- day, when the first round m-as decided, the three Warrington teams were victorious. Indeed, the ,Cemetery Hotel contingent, who met the 'Bird-in-Hand at Flixton, set up a. record score of 252, notf a man failing to account for his opponent. They won by a majority of 158, only three of the opposition scoring double figures, and one of these only got more than 11. That was F Smith, whom T. Ellison disposed of by 21-19.

At the George Hotel, Prestwich, Bull's Head gained a narrow victory over Leigh Liberals by 14 points. There are some of the best players in. the Bull's Head team, and W. Jolley, A. Gleave (No. 1), W. Daniels, IL Eyes, T. Rigby, W. Andrews, and A. Regan were largely respon- sible for the success of the team.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
C4(4(1040

Winning seven out of the dozen games against Chorlton, the Junior Conservatives did excep- tionally well. A. °leave and W. Blackledge played their first game in the County Competi- tion, and won 21-2 and 21-12 respectively. The Juniors have much cause for congratulation, and it is to be hoped this success augurs well for the future although it is rather hard that in the second round .they should be drawn against the Cemetery Hotel team on the green of Wigan Limited. Bull's Head meet Leigh Railway and should have little difficulty at Stretford.

KING AND QUEEN. H. Higham won the opening main at the King and Queen Club, Padgate-lane, on Saturday, beating W. Ashbrook 15-9 in the final. There was g. record entry of 76 competitors, and the semi-finalists were H. Higham, W. Ashbrook, A. Jolley, and J. Thompson.

CHAMPIONSHIP SCORES. The name of the member of the winning team is printed first in each game.

Cemetery Hotel v. Bird-In-Hand. Cemetery Hotel won by 158 points. W. J. Woods, 21; G. W. Batten, 8. W. Barton, 21; A. Halsall, 11. R. Antrobus, 21; W. Shepherd, 5. N. Fielding, 21; W. Shaweross, 9. CRICKET.

WARRINGTON TEAMS WIN.

Good Performances With the Bat.

A dual victory was secured over Ashton-under- Lyne on Saturday, both the first and second teams of, the Warrington Club gaining wins. They gave a fair exhibition with the bat. Thanks to the bowling prowess of Hann= and Bennett, the first team were able to get all the Ashtonians out iii. the game at Arpley for 73. The highest total made by one Ashton, bat was 24, and only two others made double figtires, which were the unlucky 13. Warrington Inftted much better and more steadily than their opponents. THE TIME TO DINE.

The old question of the right time for feeding is .raised once again in an interesting paper by Professor Bergonie, of Bordeaux, read before the Academy of Science, says the "Evening Standard." The scientist states that as a result of his experiments with regard to the expendi- ture of energy, by human beings during the 24 hours, the first substantial repast should be taken between half-past seven and eight o'clock in the morning. The second should be ready at about eleven o'clock at night, or even later. A light meal between three and four in the after- noon would also be allowed by Professor Ber- . . gome.

Mayor Vacates the Ch air as a Protest.

COUNCILLOR GOUGH'S ATTACK.

The Council meeting on Tuesday was brought, to an abrupt and exciting close through the Mayor leaving the chair as a protest. Such an incident has never before occurred in the history of the Council. • The meeting was about ten minutes in progress when Councillor Gough started to speak against a resolution of the Paving Committee, and made what the Mayor characterised as a personal attack upon a Councillor. Councillor Gough was asked to resume his seat, and as he refused to do so the Mayor imme- diately left the chair and the chamber. The Deputy-Mayor, Dr. Joseph, was voted to the chair. He sought to patch up the difficulty by asking Councillor Gough if he would withdraw and apologise. Councillor Gough said he would withdraw, but would not apologise. ' This brought the proceedings to a deadlock, and the Deputy-Mayor moved that the meeting be adjourned. Alderman Tinnion seconded, and the meeting came to an end. It lasted 15 minutes, five of which were spent on the "scene."

For five minutes one of the most exciting in- cidents ever connected with the Town 'Council held the attention of the members, and general amazement was expressed at the sharp passage of arms which took place. between the Mayor and Councillor Gough. The matter arose over the minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Com- mittee. The minutes contained the following resolution:—

That the cross streets shown on the plan submitted for the erection of houses near Westy-lane for Mr. Gordon Roberts and others, be paved with tarred macadam. ALDERMAN TINNION'S VIEWS. Alderman TizmoN, in moving approval of the minutes, said: I was not at the meeting when this resolution was passed, but I have been ac- quainted with these particular streets, and while I am chary about making a departure, still the committee have carefully considered the matter and passed the resolution. These are undoubtedly secondary streets, and so long as the material used is of that nature which has given satisfaction elsewhere and has the ap- proval of surveyors, I see no objectfiSn in pass- ing this resolution. Personally, I should have preferred that the question should not have arisen at the present time. Councillor PgMBERTON seconded. Councillor Gomm": As chairman are you pre- pared to withdraw this resolution? The MAYOR: No; he has just moved confirma- tion of the resolution.

COMMENCEMENT OF THE "SCENE." Councillor GOUGE(: Then, Mr. Mayor, I move that this resolution be not confirmed. lam somewhat astounded—l could hardly believe what I saw on the agenda paper. The chairman only a month or two since fought -against the property owners getting this concession granted. lie said it was a had policy, and that furthez cost of re-paving would fall upon the Corpora. tion. Now I find here.a resolution which•is-an. exception, a favour to one of the bosses of the, Slate Company—Councillor Roberts's son. (Loud cries of "Order.") The MAYOR; Councillor Gough, don't be per- sonal! •

Councillor Govan: I want you only to recol- lect the last time that Councillor Roberts made an ungentlemanly attack on Councillor Pem- berton, he was never interfered with.

Councillor WILKINSON: I would fetch a police- man.

The DEPUTY-MAYOR: In support of the Mayor I think it is only right that we should take the course I have named. That seems to me to be the most convenient and direct way. Alderman Tpuriox : I second the adjournment. I do think the time has come when we as a Council should support the Mayor or Deputy- Mayor. The time has come. I think, when the dignity of this chair should be upheld—(general cries of " Hear, hear")—and as a protest against the conduct of certain members in absolutely disregarding the chair, I think that this meet- ing ought to• adjourn. An adjournment is one of those ways by which we can show that it is most important that proper and due regard should be paid to the order of debate. In the interest of order I second ' the motion the Deputy-Mayor has made.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INCIDENT DEVELOPS.

The lettroa : I didn't notice it or else he would have been. Councillor Gomm This firm gets all the plums from the Corporation. (Cries of "Order.")

The MAYOR, who had been persistently ring- ing the bell: There is no firm mentioned here. Councillor- Gouoit proceeded to ask a question. regarding the Slate Company amid cries of "Order" and "Chair" and a successful attempt of the Mayor to drown Councillor Gough's voice by continually ringing his bell. The MAYOR: Councillor Gough, you must sit down. I have called you to order, and you have disobeyed me. Councillor Got•un : I am sorry—. Are you favouring Councillor Roberts? (" Order.")

~ YOU...MUST SIT DOWN."

- The MAYOR: lam favouring no one. You must sit down, Councillor (lough. Councillor Gorari: It looks very like favour- ing— The rest of the sentence was drowned in cries of " Chair" and ".Order."

_ - - The MAYOR: Will you sit down, Councillor Gough? Councillor COUGH: No! The MAYOR: If you won't, then, gentlemen, I shall leave the chair.

The Mayor then left his seat. Councillor WILKINSON : I should not leave the chair. I should get somebody in to shift him out. Alderman S3IETRURST : Councillor Gough has sat down and we may get the Mayor to come back.

MAYOR DECLINES TO RETURN.

Two members went to see the if7l;or, who de- clined to return. The Town Clerk proposed that the Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph) take the chair, and this was carried unanimously. Dr. JOSEPH (taking the chair) said : Under such special circumstances I consider it my

duty to move the adjournment of this Council meeting.
