IN THE COURTS. BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Aldermen Bolton and James Evans, Messrs. G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, W. Roberts, T. Locker, and W.

urness. - Reflection on the Mirrors.—Elizabeth Lavin, 24, Portland-street, was remanded for seven days on charges of stealing two mirrors, value 28., the property of some person unknown, and two earthenware dishes, value is. 9d., the property of Thomas Oultram, 7, Kendrick-street—It was stated that the prisoner went to Mrs. Humph- ries, 87, Buttermarket-street, and offered to sell the two mirrors, saying that her children were starving. Mrs. Humphries had purchased articles from the woman before, but in conse- quence of information received she communi- cated with the police. Prisoner denied in toto all knowledge of the mirrors. Whilst inquiries were being made, the question of the dishes arose. In order that inquiries could be made a remand was necessary.—Prisoner: They are my own glasses; I bought them. Licence Transfers.—The following transfers were granted by the magistrates: Off-licence, 18, Priestley-street, from William Henry Jones to James Alfred Jones; off-licence, 24, Warburton- street, from Henry Tavlin to Thomas Roberts; Roebuck Hotel, Bridge-street, from Mary Ann Chrimes to William Ashton; British Tar Win- wick-road, from Arthur Moffatt to Fred Walker White; Lower Angel Hotel, from Charles Wil- liam Corbett (deceased) to Christopher Daniel McMillen.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

Beforb Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), Mr. C. Jackson Holmes and Mr. W. Roberts.

Alleged Child Neglect.—. John Cope, 4, Plump- ton-street, was summoned for neglecting his children.--Mr. A. Browne appeared for the N.S.P.C.C., and Mrs. Cope, bearing out his opening statement, said she had six children, one of which was a cripple. She earned 18s. per week. Twelve months ago she was compelled to leave her husband, who promised- to pay her ss. per week. He now owed her 518. He was always drunk.—Defendant said he had always paid the money when he had got it.--After other evidence had been given by a daughter of the parties and Inspector Wainwright, the case was adjourned for four weeks to see if defendant paid anything to his wife.

WEDNESDAY. "Wronged in Law."—Jessie Jackson, a domes- tic servant, whose parents reside at Soapery- I road, Paddington,- was, charged with between April 22 and April 26 stealing a pair of earrings from a room at 52, Buttermarket-street, value 48., the property of Ann Hankinson. She was further charged with on April 6 stealing from a coat pocket in the dwelling-house, 22, Butter- market-street, the sum of 10s. Ofd. in cash and a purse, the property of Agnes Wheelan.—ln- spector Williamson said that prisoner was em- ployed by Mrs. Hankinson, from whom she stole the earrings, which were found in her pocket. The money was taken from the pocket of the charwoman.—Mr. A. Browne, who de- fended, submitted that the offence was technic- ally stealing. The prisoner did not take the earrings away, and the money she only changed. Nobody was wronged actually only in law.— 'Prisoner was placed under probation for 12 , months. Market Thefts.—Prudence Kean, 1, Philip- street, was charged on three counts with steal- ing from stalls in the Market-place on Satur- day. Supt. Williamson stated that pris- oner had only been in Warrington two months, although she had lived in the town previously with her husband, who WAS a Warrington man. The prisoner was observed to take two articles, the subject of one charge, and when she was searched the others were found in her posses- sion. Nothing was known against the prisoner, who was placed under probation for twelve months.

Amusing Himself.—Arthur Tyrall, a middle- aged man, of no fixed abode, was charged with being drunk and disorderly.—lt was stated that prisoner was pulling a can along the street by a milerah.Catrrueeeitt! a to piece of string.—lle was fined 55., with the alternative of seven days. AI d caarteHro,rswea7Tcahmaergs rf working a horse in an unfit condition, and Arthur Lunt, contractor, Knutsford-road, was charged with causing the horse to be worked.— The evidence showed that a police officer stopped the driver of the horse, as the animal was pass- ing over Warrington Bridge, and told him to take it out of the shafts.—lnspector Dee stated that the horse was unfit to work.—Mr. A. Browne, for the defence, held that the animal had been resting for six weeks, and Mr. Lunt only sent it out for exercise. The load it was pulling was not a big one. The animal had passed over the bridge several times the pre- vious day, and also on the day of the charge, but nothing had been said about its condition.— The case was dismissed on payment of costs.

===== 0002601_19140509_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
EFFORT AT PEACE.

Councillor HENSHALL: Is that the only course that can be adopted? It is very unfortunate if the business of the Council is to be stopped. I was wondering if we had not another way of acting. Does Councillor Gough realise—l won't say the gravity of his offence, as I want to be a peacemaker—the way the Mayor has taken it? Could Councillor Gough see his way to with- draw and apologise?z• _

The DrirrrfifAreit : If Councillor Gough would withdraw---

Councillor HENSHALL : He would appreciate the feelings of the Council. It would be very un- fortunate if—

The Dreuvr-MAvort : I think Councillor Gough ought to withdraw and apologise unreservedly to the Mayor. Councillor Gouas: I don't want to stop the debate of the Council or the work of the Coun- cil. It has met here for a specific purpose—to carry on the business of the town—and I am prepared under these circumstances to with- draw.

MEETING COMES TO A CLOSE. - The DEPUTY-MAYOR; I think you ought to 'withdraw and apologise. (Hear, hear.) , Councillor Gomm: I cannot. I have done no wrong. Councillor HENSTIALL Suppose we put it to the Mayor that he withdraws? (Cries of " No.") The DEPUTY-MAYOR: It should be an unre- served apology—no half measures. He has de- fied the chair. _

Councillor CRAix : Considering that Council- lor Gough does not think fit to apologise. I don't think that we have any other course open to us out of respect to the Mayor but to adjourn the meeting. (General cries of "Hear, hear.") Alderman BENNETT : It is a matter of gentle- manly feeling on the part of Councillor Gough. If he exercised that gentlemanly feeling which commends itself to most of us lie would apolo- gise, but if he has not that feeling_we cannot put it into him. It seems to me that the sub- stantial justice of the case would be met by a withdrawal, because if the remark is with- drawn .it is as though it had never been made, ,to all intents and purposes. I think we. might be satisfied with the withdrawal, leaving to Councillor Gough the odium of having' refused to do the gentlemanly thing. On a vote being taken the meeting was de- clared adjourned, there being 17 in favour and none against. COST OF SCHOOL REPAIRS.

_ _ ..—.—...—. Only two or _three items of business were transacted before the meeting came to an abrupt end as reported above. Councillor HALLI WELL asked Councillor Roberts (chairman of the Education Committee): (1) What is the total cost for repairs up to date for the Beamont Council School? (2) What has been the total cost for repairs up to date for the first Bolton Council School?

Councillor ROBERTS answered that the cost of the repairs for Beamont School for _ the six years ending 31st March, 1914, was £lBB Bs. sdo, 'and for the Bolton School £77 7s. Of the sum of £77 7s. there was an item of £2O, Bs. 9d. for 'repairs to the school yard, which was in the contract of another firm. He wished to be just to the builder as far as his contract was con- cerned, so that the actual cost was less by .220 Bs. 9d. Did that satisfy Councillor Iralliwell? Councillor HALLIWELL : Yes, it's quite satisfac- tory. THAT DEPUTATION AGAIN.

-•-••• .... Councillor ARCHER asked what had taken place about the deputation on school accom- modation who were to go to London to interview the Board of Education. Councillor ROBERTS said he was sorry there had been a misunderstanding about the matter, and nothing had been done. It was the General Purposes Sub-committee of the Education Com- mittee which should have been approached, and not the General Purposes Committee of the Cor- poration. He- would see that that was done.

EDUCATION ACT, 1902. SECTION 8 (1) & (3). COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. LOCAL EDUCATION AUTHORITY. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN in accordance with the provisions of Section 8 (1) and (3) of the Education Act, 1902, that the Council of the County Borough of Warrington, being the Local Education Authority for the purposes of Part 111. of that Act, propose to accept a trans- fer to them under the provisions of the Elemen- tary Education Act, 1870, of the SILVER-STREET WESLEYAN SCHOOL, situated in Silver-street, in the County Borough of Warrington. The 'School will be available for the following area : Parts of the ORFORD, , ST. JOHN'S and BEWSEY WARDS. T. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk and Clerk to the Local Education Authority. Dated Bth May, 1914. The Managers of any existing School, or any ten ratepayers in the area for which it is pro- posed to provide the School, may appeal against this proposal by a letter addressed to the Secre- tary, Board of Education, Whitehall, London, S.W. which should reach him within three 11iontliS from the date of this notice.

WARRINGTON UNION. TO BUILDERS, CONTRACTORS, &c. The Guardians invite Building Tenders for the erection and completion of NEW BOARD ROOM, OFFICES, &c., on a plot of land abut- ting on Winmarleigh and Museum-streets, War- rington. Applications for quantities to be made to me, the undersigned, with a remittance of Two guineas, which amount will be refunded on receipt of a bona-fide Tender. The rate of wages paid for labour through- out this work is to be at the usual standard rate of union wage in the district. The Architects, 3fessrs. W. and S. Owen, Cairo-street, Warrington, will supply any in- formation as regards the building work. Applications for quantities must be received by me not later than Saturday next, the 16th May, 1914. The Guardians do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. By order, ARTHUR BOTTOMLEY, Clerk. Union Offices, Bewsey Chambers, Warrington Bth May, 1914.

CONGREGATION 108 YEARS OLD. Anniversary Services .at St. John's Church. One hundred and eight years have gone by since St. John's congregation came into exist- ence, and four years since the present church was erected. As it is also 60 years since St. John's was admitted within the English Presby- terian Synod, arrangements are being made for diamond jubilee celebrations later in the cur- rent year. On Sunday. however, the annual dedication festival is to be held, the special preacher being the Rev. Alexander Wylie Blue, of Sunderland, and late of Glasgow. FOOTBALL. RYLANDS' GREAT RECORD. HAVE NOT LOST A LEAGUE MATCH Rylands' have had a record season. In all they have played 26 matches and only lost four, the last being that against the Rest of the League at Cadishead on Saturday. The following details show the remarkabh record they have set up in the Manchestei League, Manchester Shield, N.U. Cup (qualify- ing round) and the Lancashire Junior Cup Com- petitions :- September 11.-Weaste (Manchester Shield), at Cadishead ; won, 24 points to 5. September 29.-Runcorn White Star (Lanca- shire Junior Cup), home; won 8-3, October 4.-Cadishead (League), home; Won 13-5. October 11.-Wardley (League), home; wail 4-3. October 18.L-Salford "A" (friendly), away; lost 8-15. October 25.-Swinton Park (League), home; won 14-2. November 1.-Swinley Hornets (2nd round L.J.C.), home; won 13-0. November 8.-Prestwich C.I. (League), away; won 5-4. November 15.-Sherdley Athletic (3rd round L.J.C.), home; won 39-0 November 22.-Pendleton (League), away; r won 9-3. November 29.-Ditton _Recs. (4th .round away; won 9-2. December 13.-Spring View (semi-final L.J.C.), home; won 16-2. December 20.-Swinton Park (League), away; won 13-0. December 25.-St. -Helens Recs. (friendly), home; won 5-3. December 27.-Pemberton Rovers (N.U. quali- fying round), home; won 21-3. January 10.-Runcorn White Star (2nd round N.U.C.), home; won 10-2. January 24.--Cadishead (League), away; won 5-.4). - January 31.-Firs Lane (3rd round N.U.C.), away ;.won 13-5. February 14.-Wigan ,Highfield (4th round N.U.C.), home; lost 8-11. February 21.-Abram Rangers (final L.J.C.), Widnes; won 11-3. March 7.-Pendleton (League), home; won 18-0. March 14.-Wardley (League), away; won 18-8." March 21.--41ixton "(League), away; won 3-0. March 28.-Flixton (League), home; won 18-2. April 4.-Salford "A" (friendly), home; lost April 11.-Clifton (League), home; won. April 18.-Clifton (League), away; won. April-Z.-Prestwich C.I. (League), home; won 'May 2.-Rest of League, Cadishead; lost. Rylands' flees.-v. Rest of League. This match was played at Cadishead before a fair crowd. Rylands' were short a Griffiths, Laws and Floyd. Stretford kicked off against a 41704 wind. A good run by Povey nearly ended in a score for the Rest, Potter saving grandly. The League still continued to press With the help of the Wind. From a pack in mid-field Warren passed to Gallop, who broke through his opponents, but when near the line passed to Brandwood, who fumbled, a dead ball resulting. Good play by Linney for the League caused Rylands' some trouble. From a pass by Oldham to Vinton saw dist player score at the corner, Linney kicking a. splendid- goal. U. Foray next seared for the League,. Linney again goaling. Half-timo arrived, with the score: League, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points); Rylands', nil. The Rest commenced the second half in good earnest, but Rylands' seemed to wake up, Morris scoring at the corner. Potter failed at goal. Morris scored again, the goal-kick failing. Vinton next scored for the League, and Linney again kicked a grand goal. Rylands' dashed to the other end, Potter scoring, hut the referee awarded a dead-ball. Potter got over at the corner, the goal-kick again failing. Considering the state of the ground, a good game ended in favour of the League by 3 goals 3 tries (19 Points) to Rylands' 3 tries (9 points). Stretford, Horrocks, Morris and Gallop were the pick of Rylands'. ANGLING. Warrington Workingmen's Associa- tion. N.A. CENTRE. THE WEAVER. The progress regarding the Weaver is working out more to the • satisfaction of all secretaries concerned, and it will not be long before we can announce, through these columns, the terms offered by the Northwich Joint Committee, and whether we in Warrington accept them. RAMBLING MATCH. Very little angling is being done at present by our members, but a revival is expected on Sunday, May 17th, when the rambling match is to take place at Mickle Trafford. The commit- tee is offering a beautiful gold and silver medal with enamel centre of an angler landing his fish. The work is by Mr. Hallett, Bridge-street. It makes a splendid first prize for the match. The Assistant Secretary will be at headquar- ters to-night and on Monday to receive names and subscriptions for the match. .A.S. GOLF. WARRINGTON SWIMMING CLUB. Preparing for the Polo Season. The Warrington Swimming Club are making, preparations for the coming season, and on ' Tuesday evening, wjaen the polo team practised in the baths, one was glad to see that all the men maintained their excellent form, and if that is anything to go by, the team should do well in the Second Division of the Liverpool Polo League. All the old favourites will be in: eluded in the team, and no doubt Pepper will prove to be the usual "hot stuff " for opponents, ' as will also the other players. The engagements will comprise Birkenhead (last year's cham- pions), Widnes (the runners-up), Runcorn, Woolton, St. Bridget's (Liverpool), Bootle, and the new club, St. Helens Coronation. The first encounter will be with Runcorn on May 28th, in the local baths. On the same night it is expected there will be a large attendance, for, in addition to the polo match, the works competition, for which there is a record entry, will also commence. The entries for this competition close on May 14th. The Warrington polo team have joined the Lancashire county, and we understand with a bit of luck we shall have the pleasure of wit- nessing a county match in the town. • THE EVOLUTION OF PARLIAMENTARY PUBLICITY. Shorthand writers first gained access to the House of Commons with some sort of official acquiescence about 1786. In the year 1803 they are to be found occupying the back bench in the Strangers' Gallery without interference from the House officials, although the latter still affected to be ignorant of their presence. In 1836 the House commenced to publish the laily division lists itself, and from that time onwards the old idea that _there was something sacred about the doings of the House which required the prohibition of publicity was exploded. After the great fire of 1834 the reporters were admitted as a matter of course to the temporary building used by the Commons, and when, on February 3rd, 1852, the representatives of the people took possession of their new chamber in the Palace of Westminster, the Press were at last officially recognised, and the,reporters' gallery, as it at present exists, was an acknowledged fact.

Couldn't Be Bone. " Well, now, really, Miss Jeerwell, I should like to hear what you would consider the ideal man," said DiMad. "Define him for me, won't you?" "I couldn't," said Miss Teerwell, with a plea- sant smile. "The terms are essentially contra- dictory." Ant Story—From America. A cook, being annoyed by the, depredations of ants, put a pie on the pantry shelf, surrounded it with a thick circle of molasses and awaited developments. They came in the shape of fully 500 red ants, led by an ant larger than any other. When he came to the molasses he halted and held a con- sultation with a small detachment from the main army. Each one selected a place where the stream of molasses was narrowest, the leader making a tour of inspection, and then all the ants mane their way to a hole in the wall, where the plastering was loose. Each ant took a tiny piece of plaster, and to and fro they went from the nail hole to the molasses for two hours until they had thrown a bridge across. Then they formed in line, marched over, and soon every member of the army was contentedly eating pie.
